All Eyes On You

by DistantStorm

Summary

Summer, 2021. Shane Hollander is Major League Hockey's hottest free agent. MLH fans and organizations alike must grapple with their expectations: Hollander is not the young man the league discarded in 2017, for better or worse. There is more to his life than hockey, even if he says frustratingly little about his boyfriend, another player known only as Lily.

When the Ottawa Centaurs lace up for the 2021-2022 season with a stacked lineup, failure is not an option. What the world believes to be the newest iteration of a longstanding rivalry just might be hockey's greatest love story.

Notes

Friendly reminder that we will be on a weekly update schedule until this story is completed, then I'll bump the updates up to a daily cadence. I will post the next chapter on April 20th so we're back on to our regularly scheduled Monday updates.

Chapter 1

September, 2017 Växjö, Sweden


Shane looped tape around his stick without having to think about what he was doing. He didn't need music or distractions to hone his nerves the way other guys did. He craved the ice, the way the sound of his blades crunching ice could wash out the roar of a crowd, the way his focus narrowed instinctively and time slowed down in a way that made him feel like he was limitless.

There were very few distractions here, which was great sometimes but often left him stuck in his own head, halfway underwater. His teammates spoke a multitude of languages, some knew English, a few knew French, and everyone communicated in hand signals and gestures to bridge the gap when language failed to convey what they meant. It was simple.

Too simple, maybe, but it was professional hockey that Shane got to play without people asking a million questions, so he would take it. Honestly, he probably ought to be more grateful. It was mostly luck that had connected him with the SHL’s commissioner while he'd been at Wimbledon, which he'd gotten to go to as Rolex’s parting gift—they’d quietly bought out his contract almost immediately afterward. If he hadn't made that connection, hadn't forced himself to say, publicly, that Montreal and the MLH no longer wanted him, he'd be sitting at home spiraling even more than he was now, in a foreign country with no friends or family on the same continent.

He ripped the tape with his teeth, the harsh sound of it ripping like a hook in his brain, pulling him out of his thoughts. The blade needed to be taped next. It only took a couple seconds, maybe five or six, filled with perfect focus and precise loops, then the tape got ripped off there, too. The loose end was tucked in with care, making it look seamless.

Someone a couple stalls over came by, making grabby hands toward his roll. He offered it without looking to see who it was. It didn't matter, the room was a brotherhood. Most things among them were shared.

He let the stick rest in the crook of his arm, momentarily thrown off by the interruption.

“Need a marker?” Mats Ceder, forever captain of the Växjö Lakers, held one out to him.

Shane took it before realizing what he’d done. Something about playing in a new place made him feel like things needed to be different. He didn't need any rituals haunting him lest some stupid superstition he’d had about skate laces convince him things in Sweden will go the way they had in North America.

He considered handing the marker back, but Mats was watching him like he was a bomb in need of diffusing. He never said anything about it, but they both knew Shane was a mess, jumpy and afraid of himself, afraid of the way others saw him, afraid that he'd get himself kicked out here, too, even if everyone told him otherwise.

It was too late not to follow through. He uncapped the marker and thought about his mom, who’d been so hell bent on him staying in Canada, maybe taking a year off or finding a team to take him at the trade deadline once things calmed down. He didn’t think she understood. She wanted to, she’d certainly tried, and she supported him even if she didn’t agree with him running off to Europe even if it was a sound plan—even his agent had agreed it was a good call, it just… didn’t fit the picture of Shane she had in her head.

Just like Shane didn’t fit in Major League Hockey. Not anymore.

It hurt. It always hurt, but thinking about it made it burn bright, a sharp ache in his chest. He missed it. The hockey, mostly, not everything else. But he was lucky: nobody in Sweden cared about the how or the why, they just cared that he was willing to suit up for them and hit the ice. His teammates didn’t care because being gay didn’t have a damn thing to do with his skill.

And he was damn good. The best, actually.

Or maybe tied for it. He guessed he’d never know for sure.

He swallowed against that thought, blinking away thoughts of pouting lips curved up into a smile when he approached the dot for a faceoff. Not that there had been much smiling, at the end. There’d been no smiling or smirking or even eye contact at the end. Just avoidance. Dismissal.

It had been Shane’s fault, and now there was nothing Shane could do. Nothing that wouldn’t make it worse, anyway. He wouldn't ruin anyone else's career, not even to assuage his broken heart.

Because it was broken. Everyone thought he was hung up on the Metros and the MLH. He was good at keeping secrets, but bad at lying. He couldn't even lie to himself, especially not about Ilya Rozanov. Not anymore. Maybe one day the ache in his chest would scar and the pain would be a hollow ache he felt on cold, rainy days instead of this perpetually red hot bleeding wound.

He looked at his stick again. The marker felt heavy between his fingers.

Well. There was something. It would never be enough and he knew Ilya would never see it, but it would have to do.

Very carefully, he wrote the name Ilya Rozanov had given himself in Shane’s phone all those years ago. Four letters: L-I-L-Y.

It felt like agony. It felt like the closest he’d ever get to telling the truth.


January, 2021
Växjo, Sweden


Ottawa Centaurs. Emergency landing. Tampa Bay.

It wasn’t even seven am and even fucking Eurosport had coverage. That's where Shane had seen it before switching to his iPad to watch ESPN, who would do more than display a headline as a footnote during reruns of football matches.

He was going to lose his mind. Had already lost it, if he was being honest with himself. There was this horrible thing inside him, this monstrous feeling he couldn't shake.

He should have fucking been there.

He probably wouldn't have been, he couldn't even fucking picture a world where he and Rozanov—fuck it, Ilya—were in an actual relationship, much less actually communicating like people in relationships supposedly did. It didn't change the way he felt like his heart was going to tear its way out of his chest before it spontaneously combusted.

Ignoring the tiny, catastrophizing voice in the back of his head that suggested he was dying—thank you hindbrain, he knew what a panic attack was, he'd had his fair share—he texted the team group chat, which was also freaking out to say he'd follow up with Derek Briggs.

The kid had been his problem, according to Mats, even though he hadn't been fooling Shane, who had given troubled vets on the Metros a rookie to mentor enough times to recognize the play.

He forced himself to get it together long enough to call, to pace through the rattling flutter in his chest until it burned low enough to call manageable. He comforted the kid, because Derek was, and Shane loved him like a brother and he deserved to have someone in his corner who understood him.

Because, really, Mats had laughed off his ass when Derek asked him and Arty not to try and hook him up with beautiful women and said I knew you were the right choice for Hollzy, which had prompted a round of blushing and stammering from the kid, then twenty-one and a little more fearful, about how he was pretty sure Shane wasn't his type, which Mats had waved away because he hadn't been playing matchmaker, he'd been trying to build community.

The North American scouting teams that had passed over Mats were imbeciles, in Shane's opinion. He could've been magic over there. If he'd landed on a team like Philadelphia or Boston, he'd have his jersey in the rafters and a statue in the square. He was a captain through and through. His skill wasn't top tier, but his effort was transcendent. He worked harder than anybody on the ice and it showed.

The phone rang for what seemed like forever. “Pick up pick up pick up,” he muttered at the ringer, followed by, “Bigrig, pick up your fucking—”

“Hey Hollzy.”

Fuck. Shane squeezed his eyes shut tightly.

“I'm okay.”

“Fuck you, I know that. I saw the news,” he was almost yelling. He needed to not be yelling. “Why the fuck did I have to learn that you almost died from ESPN? Why didn't you call me?”

“I didn’t want to wake you up in the middle of the night to say I didn’t die,” Derek admitted. “Little too close to home. Plus, what if I got on the team bus and it crashed between the airport and hotel?”

Shane pulled his phone away from his face. He could hear the shaky breathing on the other end of the line. This wasn't about him. Derek needed him to be strong, and Shane could do that. He could freak out and spiral later.

“Tell me you're not alone right now.”

“No, Mom, I'm not alone,” came the begrudging, yet dutiful answer, Derek explaining his situation, the younger players all bunking together rather than toughing it out in pairs.

He was glad.

Admittedly, he'd been concerned when Derek was noticed. Not because Derek wasn't good. He was fantastic, actually, a great utility forward who could adjust to play up and down a lineup with ease. Shane wanted him to go far.

He just worried about the League’s politics and Derek's very specific policy of not concealing his sexuality, even if he didn't advertise it. Part of Shane wished he'd been that brave, had just let it happen, the truth popping up unannounced and being what it was. Would it have been better for him? He wasn't sure.

He had been less concerned when he found out it was the Centaurs doing the scouting. Ilya Rozanov was their captain and he didn't tolerate bullshit. It was one of the things Shane lo—liked about him. Derek had shared little tidbits about his new team in their catch-up calls, about how nobody really knew why Rozanov had left Boston even though it had been years, or how he'd bag skated them within an inch of their life the morning after a sloppy win but had convinced their coach to make a morning skate optional after a loss where they tried.

He did his best to reassure Derek, stayed with him until his other teammate came back, grateful for the accidental scrap of information Derek had shared, his Cap made sure, because of course Ilya would.

Time and space from the League, from everyone and everything he'd ever fucking known, had given Shane clarity. So much fucking clarity. He could see the way Ilya bent over backwards for others, the way he had that one afternoon in 2016 for Shane, so desperate to make the things they couldn't have normal that they'd tipped the other way. He recognized the mask of arrogance and confidence and the way Ilya tried, the only way he knew, to let himself be known.

He was pretty sure he would never forget the way Ilya had seemed so cool as he backpedalled (Hollander, it isn't a big deal), how it had taken Shane way too long to figure out he was panicking too, the both of them watching a relationship that wasn't slipping like sand through their fingers.

He closed his eyes against the grief, breathed through it. He hoped Ilya had gone to bed with someone, even if he hated the idea of it, or that some other rookie or player came to him, seeking the comfort of a teammate. He hated the idea of Ilya being alone.

He hated that he had to be okay with it being someone else, no matter how many times he made himself think about it because ultimately, he'd done this to them both.

Ilya probably hated him, anyway. Plenty of people did. Not everyone could be Hayden, who knew even less than Ilya but still messaged him often, like he could somehow will Shame back into his life with pictures of the kids and messages that almost always made Shane cry.

He used to think a lot more about being selfish, saying fuck it and open up that closed door. It had been so bad in the beginning, the way the news had spun his departure with less than nothing to go on. It was bad enough that his parents had been forced to change their phone numbers and stay with family abroad. He couldn't pull Hayden and his family into that. It would've ruined him more than the trade Montreal had forced him into.

Three years on, it probably wouldn't be as bad, but really, there was no point. He liked Sweden, liked being able to travel around Europe when time allowed, liked his teammates. It was a job he was good at. Maybe this wasn't where he'd imagined them raising his jersey to the rafters but that was the funny thing about life.

It was messy and imperfect and sometimes you just didn't get what you wanted.

Shane pulled up to the rink, waved hello to the usual attendant who humored his attempts to speak Swedish which he could understand better than he'd ever speak, and pulled into his parking spot between Benny and Mats who had kids and usually came in closer to nine.

He checked his phone before he got out of the car. It was just habit at this point, the curse of a digital age, as his dad liked to say. He hadn't thought anything about it, flicking through the usual junk emails and sport alerts that made up most of his notifications until there was only one left.

Instagram
You have new unread messages.

If someone asked him why he tapped instead of swiped, Shane would say it was instinct. He was a professional hockey player. Gut instinct was the difference between going home or winning a championship, was the place where talent and skill were forged into something with meaning. It was the override that lit up his synapses and stopped him from swiping to dismiss the messages that would change everything.

Chapter 2: Interlude: Yuna

Chapter Notes

For all the beautiful people wanting Yuna POV: I saw you, I already had this written, and was just trying to figure out where in the story it made most sense to include (it was almost its own one-shot, but I think this works better).

Anyway you have excellent taste. >:)

January, 2021
 Ottawa, ON


Yuna Hollander, for as long as she lived, would never ever forget the call. Shane had called her back to back to back, so much that her phone had kicked on, the ringer she had set for him shrill and sharp, meant to draw her attention in loud or crowded rooms.

She was awake instantly.

“Shane?” Her voice, gruff with sleep, sounded muddy and confused. She was not.

“M-mom?”

Someone was dead, she thought. Someone had to be dead or dying or worse. She’d never heard Shane’s voice wobble this way. Not even when the Metros or the League had done the horrible things they had done to her boy. Sure, he’d cried and shouted and been upset, but it was nothing compared to this, to the devastation in his voice, the way his breaths shook like each one of them was a herculean effort.

“I’m here, baby,” she said. “What’s going on?”

“I-I,” he swallowed. It was loud and damning, not the careful, polished way she’d taught him to present himself, “I can’t,” he said. “Do this.”

“Do what?” She tried her best to keep her voice low and calm, to ignore the growing part of her that was seconds away from freaking out. She threw the blankets off, waving away David, who was blinking up at her in the dark with sleepy eyes, having reached for and flicked on the lamp. He nodded and headed out of their bedroom, presumably to the kitchen to start their pot of coffee.

The wall clock said three fifty-six am.

“Shane?”

“It’s—I can’t,” he repeated, just shy of hyperventilating. “I’m fucking—I fucked all of it up, Mom.” Then, belatedly, “Shit. ’M sorry.”

She wasn’t sure which part of this he was apologizing for: the swearing, the middle of the night wake-up call, but it didn’t matter. Her sweet, mindful boy wouldn’t call her for nothing, and she could hear his hurt. “Are you safe? Where are you?”

“‘m at the rink,” He murmured. “In the parking lot.”

“But you’re safe?”

She could hear his palms bash against the steering wheel, bam-bam-bam, then the sound of a car door, muffled, then another door opening and a soft, “Hey Hollzy, watch the mitts,” followed by Shane hiccoughing away from the speaker and then, “Want me to stay or—okay, I’ll wait for you by the doors. Flash the indicators if you want me to come back, yeah?”

Despite trying her best not to despise the Swedish league for being second rate, she really couldn’t argue the culture. It was significantly better. Shane’s teammates, the few she had met, were good guys. There was still homophobia, she wasn’t sure it would ever be eradicated completely, but it wasn’t in the room, and it wasn’t tolerated by management or the league.

“Was that Mats?”

“Benny,” he corrected. “Mats would be in the passenger seat right now trying to hold my hand or something.” The image was enough to make him huff.

It lessened the feeling of a fist squeezing her heart just enough to suck in a breath and hold it before she talked.

“As he should.” She wished she was there, that she could wrap him up in a hug and hold him until his tears ran out and was ready to talk. All she could do now was pull out tidbits of information piece by piece and hope she didn’t overwhelm him with the full picture before he was ready to see it for himself.

“Mom, I um,” he sniffed. “I need to stop running.” He stopped long enough for the words to trickle like melting ice down Yuna’s spine.

She had waited years for him to be ready. Had listened to David when he said not to push, had forced herself to accept that Shane was a fully grown adult they had raised well and could trust to make his own decisions, even if she didn’t necessarily agree with them.

“Can you help me?”

That was a rhetorical question. Had to be. She had binders and back up hard drives with all his league paperwork and conversations, detailed notes and spreadsheets of how it had all gone so, so wrong. It had been her final act as his manager, before he’d fired her not because he didn’t want her involved, but because he had tried so hard to tighten the blast radius so that only he would face the fallout.

(As if she would want to be a manager for anyone else.)

Her brave, honorable boy. He hadn’t been able to spare them entirely, but new phone numbers and a chunk of the summer spent abroad, first in Europe, then with David’s cousins in Portland had kept them out of the hot seat well enough.

She’d been waiting years for him to say the word, but she hadn't expected it to hurt. “Of course, Shane.”

The smell of coffee called to her like a siren song from the kitchen. She wrapped her robe around her waist and emerged from the bedroom to find David pulling down two mugs. The whole time, she listened to Shane breathe. She knew the cadence of it, knew there were tears pouring down his face, that his emotions, so carefully controlled, so frequently overlooked, were overwhelming him.

“You said you need to stop running,” she began carefully, ignoring the way David stared at her, his clearwater eyes bright with emotion, like he’d sensed this had been it the entire time. “Do you know what you want?”

“I want to come home.”

Home could mean so many things, but home was here, and not there, and for Yuna—no, for a start, she told herself sternly—that was enough.

“Okay,” she said, a little shakier than she’d liked. She tried again. “Okay. I can work with that.”

Shane sniffed over the phone in response.

“Let me call some people, okay? It’s a little early, but I can think of a couple firms who would be happy to take a look at everything and give you an idea of what they think. We need to find out if you should press charges or try to handle it under the table.” She waited, expecting him to say what he always did when she suggested it.

I can’t, like, sue the league or whatever. They’d never take me back. I’ll be a laughing stock. You heard them. I’d be ruined.

“Okay,” he said. His voice was quiet and raw, but without the tremulous flutter of panic.

“You’re certain? If we—you,” she revised, because this was his fight. She could be in his corner, but it had to be him. He’d sent himself to Sweden, and only he could bring himself back. “If you do this…”

“I can’t take it back,” he finished for her. “I know,” he exhaled. “I need to do this, Mom. I have to.”


June, 2021
Ottawa, ON


For a man who hated social media and refused to get involved with anything vaguely resembling a trend, Yuna’s husband David was very often in the right place at the right time because he tended to stumble upon news before she did half the time. She got him back with an uncanny ability to predict the future—see also, reasons why David referred to her as a witch (lovingly, of course, David didn’t have a mean bone in his body)—which she used to flaunt each spring when MLH playoffs started.

Key words: Used to.

These days? Not so much.

It was hard to muster enthusiasm for the league that had single-handedly ruined her son’s life, for the national team that had passed him by for the Olympics because it was run by the same awful people, for the world for thinking they had the right to judge. So no. She didn’t bust out her usual predictions. New York would win the fucking playoffs, but Yuna wouldn’t bother watching a league that couldn’t be assed to do right by her son.

Shane might have the same gentle, forgiving heart his father did, but Yuna’s was wrapped in thorns, dead set on protecting herself and those dear to her. When Shane became a free agent on July first, she would fall in with whatever team he chose and support them just as hardcore as she supported any of the teams Shane played for. She’d probably never love the league or any franchise as much as she’d once loved the Montreal Metros, but you know what they say: Fool me once, shame on you. Fool me twice, shame on me. Yuna wasn’t the type to let it get to three times, thank you very much.

Anyway, back to David.

On this particular Sunday morning, like most mornings, he sat at the kitchen table, readers perched low on his face, coffee at his elbow, left earbud in so he could watch youtube or the news and still hear whatever she was saying as she puttered around their cottage.

“Did you know,” David began, slowly looking up from whatever he was looking at, “That Shane went to Boston this weekend?”

Yuna held back the bitter commentary that sometimes threatened to leak out at times like these. Shane didn’t tell her a lot of things anymore. Somewhere along the way, he’d started keeping secrets.

“No, but Hayden’s there and the Raiders are out of the playoffs, so it’s probably safe to talk to him now. It's not far.”

Even though she was barely involved in Shane’s career these days—and whose fault was that, Major League Hockey, she thought angrily for about the ten millionth time—it didn’t mean she lived under a rock. She might not sit in on mediation, but she’d been the one to refer Shane to the best attorneys money could buy, and was in close contact with him and his agent as things developed.

If anything, she was less of a manager and more of a consultant.

She flicked her hands down at the sink, little splatters disrupting the top, sudsy layer of soap. She’d picked up bread baking—it was kind of annoying as far as hobbies went, especially after a loaf had gone into the oven and there was nothing left to do but hope it had risen enough, that her cuts would look artsy and intentional, then clean up the kitchen because there was no way to prevent flour from getting everywhere. She'd tried.

Maybe July first could come a little sooner, she thought begrudgingly. The sad little loaves of sourdough she made were the eighth or ninth iteration of trying to find something productive to do with her free time in the years since she'd stopped being Shane's manager.

It wasn’t like she didn’t know about what was going on. Shane still asked for help every once in a while, and Yuna watched SportsCentre to see what they were saying about Shane, what information some insider had leaked from the MLH side of things. It was a far cry from managing brand deals with some of the top brands in sport, and it killed her not to be in New York, amidst the whirlwind of legal counsel Shane had hired to handle things, but she understood why things were the way they were.

Technically, it was more healthy this way, even if part of her would always be a little desperate to be more involved. They talked more, were more open than they had been when their relationship had been weighed down with business. Her sweet little boy had always been so desperate to please and so terrified of letting people down, but in the end it had been unavoidable. He had jumped through everyone else’s hoops, even her own at times, but it hadn’t been enough. It still made her blood boil to think of those haughty fucking suits trying to snuff out her son’s talent for being honest about himself, who he was.

Joining David at the kitchen table, bowls abandoned to soak, she waited for him to share details.

He didn't disappoint. “There are pictures of him and Hayden. TMZ grabbed them from Instagram.”

“Of course.”

David held out the tablet, which she took, eyes hungrily scanning the article to see what the scavengers had written about it.

It was about as expected. The article itself was a nothingburger, more or less a link to Hayden’s instagram post. Hockey players spotted at breakfast? Big deal. Hayden hugging Shane, captured in a candid? Expected. A heartfelt, if somewhat clumsily worded caption about loving Shane like a brother no matter what? textbook, really.

She tapped on the link to Hayden’s instagram to see what other people were saying. Most of it was equally as rote, fans freaking out either positively or negatively (mostly positively, she noted with satisfaction) and a very small handful of their former teammates posting emojis that failed to convey anything of substance.

David, sensing he was not going to get his device back anytime soon, came around the table to sit beside her. She was already tapping on Hayden’s story, wondering what he wasn’t sharing with the entire world. Thankfully, despite how things are played out, she and David had always had access to Hayden and his wife’s more personal posts. She liked being able to see what their kids were up to, watching them grow into little people with stark personalities. They were probably the closest she’d ever get to grandbabies, after all, especially now that Hayden and Shane were on speaking terms again.

The first two images in his stories were just repeats of the post, nearly a day old. The third was where it got interesting. It was a photo taken by someone sitting on the other side of the table, Shane and Hayden side-by-side in their seats, a third person crouched between them so their heads were all in frame. She paused, showing it to David.

“Is that Cliff Marleau?” She asked, pausing it so they could inspect the picture more clearly. None of the photos had captions. “They look happy.”

David dutifully pulled out his phone to look him up. “That does look like Marleau,” he agreed carefully.

“Do you think he’s—”

“Maybe?” he didn’t let her finish. “I don’t think it’s a good idea to speculate, Yuna.”

For all that they did talk about, for how much healthier her relationship with Shane was now that the business side of it was minimal, there was just one thing that had never come up. A big thing. Something she tried very, very hard not to get upset at him for hiding from them for what had turned out to be years.

Maybe upset was the wrong word. She had to remind herself sometimes that Shane was an adult and, as such, she wasn’t entitled to every detail of his personal life. She wanted to support him, and it felt like she wasn’t allowed to.

And it sucked that she had to learn from Hayden Pike that Lily had been a recurring character in his life for a fucking decade.

She tapped to go to the next post and froze. “What the hell is Rozanov doing in Boston?”

“He was their captain for years,” offered David, engrossed in whatever he was doing on his phone that was not looking up Cliff Marleau’s dating history like she’d hoped. “He and Marleau were close.”

“So you are on his Wikipedia page?” She demanded.

“No, I have this thing called a brain and I use it to remember things.” He turned his head, looking at Yuna over his glasses before giving in and looking down at the screen.

She continued, “Maybe he’s trying to support Marleau? I mean, if Marleau is Lily, it would explain Hayden calling on his behalf, maybe they’re close and this was a good opportunity. Good cover too, so they could be seen together in public.”

It certainly made sense in Yuna’s head. Cliff Marleau didn’t look like the kind of person Shane would be interested in, not that they really talked about his taste in men—Shane always got so defensive, but Marleau—Cliff was attractive. Rougishly handsome, she supposed. Maybe Shane liked that. He was a heart and soul player, not the most talented, but Shane had never really pictured himself as being better than anyone. He just loved the game. Maybe that was why it worked?

She frowned as she realized something else. “Do you think Shane would play in Boston?”

David pulled the ipad from her grasp. “Hey, I was using that.”

“Yuna,” he said calmly, “It’s not Cliff Marleau.”

“You don’t know that for sure.”

His shoulders hitched in the world’s tiniest shrug, a silent, okay, have it your way and be wrong. She stopped herself from snarking back at him and fixed him with an equally silent look instead.

“Look at this picture.” His eyebrows rose slightly, suggesting he meant business. “I’m serious.”

“I already did!” She snatched it from his hands and made a show of complying with his request. It was a selfie of the four of them and their breakfasts. Hayden and Marleau were looking at the camera, but Rozanov’s head was turned, his face in profile, like he hadn’t quite been ready for the photo. Shane’s mouth was open slightly, like he’d been speaking as Hayden snapped the picture, his eyes bright with humor or happiness, a spark of emotion that very rarely translated into the digital medium. He usually looked so flat or boyish, but not here. He looked engaged and handsome, leaning forward like the others, fully invested in whatever was happening at that moment.

It took her a second to figure out what Rozanov was beaming at. It wasn't the camera, obviously, nor the massive plate of pancakes in front of him. He was smiling at Shane, whose fork was a blur of motion, encroaching upon Rozanov's plate like he meant to poach one of the blueberries swimming in butter and maple syrup atop the short stack, completely ignoring his own omelette and the seeded toast plated beside it.

“You don't think—” she shook her head, disbelieving. “They hate each other.”

“Do I think that our son, who is even more competitive than his mother,” David said warmly, a note of challenge in his voice, “Would fall in love with the person that challenged him the most?” He shrugged and let the words speak for themselves. “You did say he told you he wants to come home,” he added cryptically. “I guess we’ll see.”

Framework shifted and resettled itself in Yuna’s brain. There had been an incident involving the Ottawa team just before Shane had decided to take action. She hadn’t thought much about it at the time, but—her fingers were already flying across the screen, backing out of Instagram and going to Safari. There were other things, too. She’d seen Rozanov talk about supporting Shane, had seen plenty of pictures from the impromptu jersey moment during the Ottawa-New York series, but at the time that just felt like an Ottawa specific narrative.

And Shane had been spending a lot of time reconnecting with Lily, which they’d taken to mean he’d gotten a hotel or something so they could have privacy… but—

“So maybe… Ottawa?” She paused. She had just finished typing Ottawa Centaurs incident January 2021 into the browser, the cursor blinking lazily at her, waiting for her to hit search. “Can they even afford him?”

“I guess we’ll see,” David repeated breezily. “Can I have my iPad back before you open a million tabs? They always sync to my phone. I plugged yours into the charger this morning, it's in the living room.”

She reluctantly relinquished control of the device in favor of retrieving hers from the coffee table in the other room. When she returned, David was back in his usual seat, earbuds out. “I can’t figure out why you’re so calm about this. Don’t you want to know?”

“Of course I do, but he’ll tell us when he’s ready,” he said, perpetually steady. She sent a prayer to the universe, not that she was particularly religious, hoping that whomever Shane’s mystery man was, even if it was Ilya fucking Rozanov, he would ground him the way David did her. “I’ll do the dishes while you get started on your investigation.”

“You know me so well,” she said, coming back to the table and accepting a kiss.

Chapter 3

June, 2021
Boston, MA


Shane ignored Ilya's sleepy murmur of “Okay, Hollander, I get up too,” tucking the blankets around him and pressing a kiss to his temple before sliding out of the guest bed. Ilya had already fallen back asleep, which was good. They'd been up so late the night before, sitting on Cliff Marleau's balcony around a fire pit that was more like a decorative light source than anything, drinking beers and catching up (or getting to know each other, in Shane and Cliff’s case). He could hear the slight whistle of Hayden's familiar half-snore from the room across the hall and smiled to himself.

He had missed Hayden almost as much as Ilya these last few years. They were the only two people (who he wasn't related to) in the life he'd had before Sweden that accepted him for what he was, who truly saw him beyond the mantle of his career. Maybe it was three people now, he decided. Marley seemed like good people. When they'd arrived yesterday morning, Ilya had thrown himself into Cliff’s arms like it was nothing, and Shane would have been jealous of the way Cliff held him so tight if it hadn't been the same way he and Hayden had clung to each other.

Friends were important. So, yeah.

There was a group chat in his phone called Boston Besties 🐻 now, which was mostly Ilya and Cliff sending emojis Shane didn't know existed while Hayden reacted with thumbs up (Cliff) or down (Ilya).

The kitchen was quiet and dark except for the light that was on over the stove. He looked around as quietly as he could, finding the coffee supplies and setting the machine on the counter to brew. There wasn't much in the way of non-dairy milk options in the refrigerator, but Shane could drink his coffee black if he had to. The hardest part of the whole affair had been finding a non-Raiders mug. There was only one, which had been dusty and tucked away in the back corner of the cabinet, that said Live, Laugh, Lobstah with a borderline frightening cartoon lobster on it.

It looked like the kind of souvenir his dad would get when they went on trips that got relegated to their cottage when the cabinets overflowed in their Ottawa house. If he hadn't purposely left his phone in the bottom of his backpack, he might have taken a picture of it to send to him.

He had only just sat down with his steaming lobster mug when the floorboards lurched quietly in the hall.

Cliff wore sleep pants and an old-looking Raiders hoodie that seemed a little overstretched and threadbare in the way comfortable clothes usually were. He jerked his chin at Shane in greeting. “Thanks for making the whole pot, Hollzy.”

“No problem.”

The sounds of coffee making—the fridge door opening and closing, milk being poured, the quiet tinkering of a stirring spoon against ceramic—were comfortable and muted, not as noticeable as the light beginning to slip over the city as the sun rose.

“Wanna take this outside? S’nice out.”

Shane grabbed his hoodie from the row of hooks near the door and threw it over his head, following Cliff out into the cool morning air.

Cliff’s building was decidedly metropolitan, an old industrial building modernized into upscale residences. The skyline still looked old, in a chic sort of way. It wasn't far from where Ilya had lived, when he had his downtown penthouse.

“So,” Cliff relaxed into the patio furniture with a groan, still more than a little beaten up from the playoffs. He thumbed at the raiders logo printed on his cup idly.

Anxiety gripped Shane instantly, so he decided to take the plunge, confront it head on. “Is this, like, some shovel talk thing?”

“What? No!” He took a drink from his mug and put a foot up on the lip of the firepit in the center of the balcony. “Ilya would hate that. Besides, that's more Svetlana's territory. She's the scary one.”

Shane hummed. When Hayden had said as much over brunch yesterday, Ilya had been delighted. It was the one thing they'd willingly agreed upon. “Okay.”

“I'm trying to figure out if I should bother with my recruitment speech or…” He studied Shane's face, looking for something. Shane wasn't sure what it was.

What? “You want to… recruit me?”

“Yeah man,” he took another sip of coffee and set the cup on a little side table with an ashtray. “I'm captain, y’know? Comes with the territory.” He shrugged a little, looking out at the city beyond their balcony. It was starting to come awake, cars and buses moving around below them as the city began its day. “We got Pike back at the deadline because we needed depth at wing, but like, anybody with half a brain knew you wouldn't start something if you couldn't finish it. So…” he shrugged again, but looked over at Shane this time. “How about it?”

Shane probably should have seen this coming. He couldn't blame Cliff for trying. If their positions were reversed, he'd try to do the same thing for his team. He pressed his lips together, thumb flicking over the seam of them, trying to figure out what to say. It was hard with people, sometimes. Shane wanted Marleau to like him, but—

It was just…

“Or are you spoken for?”

His chest lurched. “What? How would I be?” He was babbling, voice quaking with anxiety. “It's been like, a day, and I've had my phone off. You're the first person who's—”

Marley waved a hand, dismissing his anxious rambling. “Dude, I'm just saying,” he reached for his mug, sloshing a couple drops of coffee out of it as he brought it around. He wiped them on his hoodie like an afterthought. “If you're already set on Ottawa—”

“I haven't decided anything,” Shane interjected. “I know it's not like, tampering or whatever because I'm not attached to a team, but I,” he forced himself to relax. “I don't know where I'm going to end up.”

The sliding door opened behind them. “Told you,” Hayden said.

“Eavesdropping, Pike?” Cliff scooched over on the little loveseat thing he was on to make room for Hayden. “Could use some help.”

“I'd love to play with you again,” Hayden told Shane earnestly, but it was in that tone of voice every player knew, the one reserved for media, for official statements and things you were required to say even if they weren't exactly what you'd wanted to.

“Me too,” Shane replied. He meant it too, but…

“It's okay, Shane. I’m really not mad or sad or whatever,” Hayden promised, and yeah. This was Hayden, not Boston Forward Hayden Pike. “If you wanna talk through it, I'm here. But," he leaned forward making sure Shane was holding eye contact before he continued. “Maybe talk to him about it too, okay? I think,” he scratched the back of his head, looking over at the door like Ilya might pop up and berate him. Cliff nudged him with his shoulder. “Just—yeah. Talk to him about it, okay? I think he's convinced you wouldn't even consider—”

“But I would.”

Hayden smiled. “Of course you would. You just haven't stopped moving long enough to let yourself think about it.”

It was, of course, true. Shane relaxed, grateful to be understood. “Fuck, I missed you, Hayd.”

“I missed you too, buddy.” Hayden reached forward and grabbed Shane's knee, squeezing it. “So,” he continued, clearing his throat and working past the emotion trying to clog it. “Ottawa?”

Now that he'd thought about it, Shane was pretty sure he'd never be able to picture anything else. He hadn't let himself think about reconciling with Ilya much less the prospect of playing on the same team with him.

“I don't think they can afford him,” Cliff said carefully. “You'd want, what? Thirteen? They pay Roz twelve point eight, plus Hayes’ extension kicks in and they need to do some moves for depth on defense. It'll be close.”

But I don't care about what I make.

The words were almost out of his mouth, but he managed to hold them back. He didn't think Hayden and Cliff would think less of him but he knew just how ridiculous it would sound coming from him. Though it hadn't been the point, at least, not for Shane, there had been a monetary component involved in his settlement with the league. Money talked and all that.

Once upon a time, before everything, he would have wanted his worth. Fifteen million would be his ask and he'd sign at thirteen point seven five and not a dollar below. He'd want a letter too, and the team that got him would need to be a contender now. He wasn't getting any younger.

It wasn't like Shane didn't want any of those things. He wanted another cup. Wanted to be a good teammate and competitor. Wanted to prove he was more than gay or Asian, that he was the best in the game and had always been, that no time away from the league could ever change that.

But money? It didn't matter. A letter? Absolutely not. He would happily be a veteran voice and mentor without it, thank you very much.

“Did he just blue screen?”

“I'm thinking,” Shane told Marleau without looking up, his gaze caught on the lip of the firepit and the blotchy pattern of gray-white ash on the metal. “Sorry, I just,” he trailed off again, distracted by the tumultuous cyclone that was his thoughts.

If he and Ilya were on the same team, how would that work? Would it be like before, that they'd need to keep their relationship a secret to their teammates? Would they have to disclose it to team management? Would they be accepting of a queer relationship between two men on their active roster? Would the rest of the roster?

Briggs had said Ilya had been good (before he'd been not so good, which was really kind of Shane's fault) and that the team was kind and supportive of him and Luca, but this could be different. Shane had history. He knew what some of the old-timer, retired players said about him after he'd gone public, that he was a drama magnet for dealing with things in the way he had.

More importantly: Did Ilya actually even want them to be on the same team? Was that why Hayden had actually suggested they talk? Did he know that Shane wanted this and was trying to talk him down gently?

The Centaurs were Ilya's franchise. Everyone knew that. Would it feel like Shane was encroaching on his territory if he signed there? Even if Shane didn't want a letter—which he really, really didn't, not anymore, never again—would it be uncomfortable or worse, a hindrance?

“What did you two say to my boyfriend?” Ilya was crouched in front of him, looking sleep rumpled and gorgeous and very, very concerned. He hadn't actually realized he’d folded over himself, head practically between his knees. “Shane, sweetheart, it is okay, yes? You're good here.”

Ilya's hand squeezed the back of his neck, solid and reassuring, but he took care not to let any other part of him touch Shane, like he worried it might be too much.

“I'm okay,” he said, but realized everyone around him seemed to think otherwise. “I'll be okay in a minute,” he revised.

Tsking, Ilya turned to Cliff and Hayden. “What did you two idiots say to him, huh? Is very delicate time—”

“Fuck you, dude, we were just trying to—”

“It's okay,” Shane lifted his head. He really didn't want Ilya to fight with their friends. “They didn't say anything they shouldn't, I just freaked out.”

“Freaked out over…” he waited expectantly for someone to tell him what was going on.

Shane mumbled some approximation of ’m gonna be a free agent, which made Ilya's expression soften.

“It will be okay,” Ilya told him, each of his hands holding one of Shane's. “I told you, every single team who isn't shitty Montreal will try to—what is that saying? Move heaven and earth?” Shane nodded. “That. They will all want you. Even fucking Montreal, who knows they do not deserve you.”

Ilya had never lied to him about hockey. So why, why did he have that look on his face, the one he'd worn when Shane got spooked and ran away, the one when he said Hollander, don't do this, the pinched one that said he wasn't telling the whole truth, that he was afraid to, that he was holding something back?

Why was it worse now, Ilya's emotions covered up with tenderness rather than frustration and avoidance? What was he so afraid of?

Was he afraid that Shane wanted to play with him or against him?

It was hard enough to figure out Ilya was afraid of something, especially for Shane, who didn't do very good with emotions at the very best of times, but there was this way Ilya's gaze went, hard but almost unfocused, even if he was looking right at you, like he was there, but not.

He was struck suddenly by the way Ilya sometimes held onto him, clinging like he couldn't stand to not be touching. It usually happened late at night or first thing in the morning, especially if Shane wasn't in bed when he woke up. He'd mutter things, mostly in Russian—Shane would learn it eventually but right now he only knew what I love you was—but sometimes, if Shane was nearing sleep he'd speak in English too.

The way he'd look then was not exactly the same as what was happening now, but it was close, and it was all Shane had to go on.

“What about Ottawa,” he whispered, not quite mustering enough oomph to make it sound like a question.

Ilya's face shuttered, and Shane was, well, he shoved past his first instinct, which was to be hurt and looked up at Cliff and Hayden who were trying to pretend they weren't stuck outside because Shane and Ilya were between them and the door. The balcony was way too small.

“Uh, maybe we should go inside and talk,” he murmured, at the same time as Ilya’s hesitant, slightly gobsmacked, “You would play in Ottawa?”

There were other things, things about being this person who Shane was now, who he didn’t used to be. Things that kind of abstractly knew about himself, that had been nebulously ordered ahead of hockey in importance. One of those things was himself as a person: he deserved to be honest with himself about who he was. The other, which he’d never said aloud before, much less allowed himself to think, was this:

“I want you more than hockey, Ilya.”

His younger self would have screamed for a concussion spotter, would have had a full-on meltdown because there was no way he’d ever love anything more than hockey, much less the asshole the league had spun into his rival, but Shane had had four very lonely years to think about it, to deal with the unpleasant sensation it had given him. Sure, he’d slept with people here and there, but it was an infrequent scratching of an itch. This understanding, this little fact about himself was quieter now, comfortable and lived in. He would play hockey on Mars if it meant Ilya would be there with him.

“You really mean that?”

There were tears overflowing from Ilya’s eyes, like someone had left a faucet to drip. They fell in perfect little drops that leaked from the corner of his eyes and cheeks. His hands, still in Shane’s, made no attempt to stop them.

”Hey,” Shane managed to free one of his own to run his knuckles under Ilya’s eyes. Getting hit with his gaze head on, eyes full of tears, was like a blow. “Hey. Of course I do,” he said seriously. “I love hockey, but it’s not as good if it’s not with you.”

”We have never played together,” Ilya protested. “You might hate it. And I will be your captain. They will not,” he trailed off, tapping his chest, English failing him, but the gesture enough to follow. He restarted a couple seconds later with a wary, “I will push you.”

Ilya had been pushing him since the moment they'd met. How could he not know that pushing Shane was what made him better?

”I don’t want a letter,” he told Ilya as Hayden muttered, “Seriously, do they talk about anything or…?”

Cliff kicked Hayden and stood. “Alright Pike, I need some Dunkin. Let’s—”

”Ohthankfuck,” Hayden blurted, words tripping over one another as he sprung to his feet.

When the sliding door closed behind them, leaving Shane and Ilya alone, Shane tugged Ilya to his feet and led him to the newly vacated two-seater.

“How are we still so bad at this?” Shane asked.

”I don’t know,” Ilya said, still teary, but with the tiniest little curl of a smile to his expression. Shane threw his arms around Ilya's shoulders, pulling him close. “Fuck.” He swiped at his face with a little more force than was necessary. “You don’t have to do this for me,” he said. “I don’t want—I will hate being apart from you, but I can manage. I will. We did it before, but it will be better this time.”

He said that, but Shane got the feeling he was saying it to convince himself and not to tell Shane it was okay. Fuck, it hurt to look at him, to know Ilya was holding things back for his sake. He didn't want that.

Shaking his head, Shane said, “I don’t want you to ‘manage,’ Ilya, I want you to be happy. I want us both to be happy.” He realized he was looking over Ilya’s shoulder instead of into his eyes and shifted his gaze. “You turned that entire team around. Anyone with half a braincell where hockey’s concerned would want to play there.”

”Was not just me,” he sniffed.

“No, but culture starts in the room, no matter what anybody else says. I know that was all you. I saw what you did in Boston. There was a reason you beat me to the cup. You made them believe way better than I ever could.”

”Your team was not as good.”

”I will concede that this one time, and if you ever bring it up again I will deny it,” he smiled and Ilya matched him. “I know everything’s like, crazy right now with me being out and a free agent and having,” he inhaled sharply, because fuck he would never be able to avoid the next part for as long as he lived, “Y’know, sued the league, but, I want to, and I know it’s really selfish but, I want everything with you. A home, a life, several cups—“

”At least three cups,” Ilya said sharply. “And then you will retire so that I can win two more and beat you.”

”Well, I guess that means the rivalry wouldn’t be dead if we played for the same team,” huffed Shane. “Do you think Ottawa would take me?”

”It would be tight,” Ilya’s cheeks, pink from crying, flattened out as his jaw worked, his expression turning a bit more reserved. “Hayes needs to be re-signed first. He needs more, Toronto was underpaying him before trade last year.” He tapped his cheek with a finger. “They’d have eleven left, maybe less, depending on Hazy’s agent. They are pretty good, I’m told.”

“I don’t need the money.”

“Ah, so you will pay for free.”

Shane shoved him. “No. Of course not.”

”So… what? I go to GM and say, ‘Shane Hollander will play for whatever money you have left?’”

“No. I want at least eight,” Shane said carefully. He’d made seven point five for years in Montreal, which was nothing compared to what he’d made in brand deals, all of that money carefully invested and saved in ways that generated enough passive income to live comfortably for the rest of his days. “Maybe eight point one?”

That little surprised smile was back, but Ilya squashed it with his words. “You are joking.”

”I’m really not.” He wasn’t. He kind of liked the idea of it. It would be a sweetheart deal, the kind that helped a team build a fucking dynasty. It was the kind of thing players did to come home, to finish their careers where they started. Even if Shane had at least another ten seasons ahead of him and would need an extension. Plenty of guys insisted on having certain numbers in their deal. Maybe Shane could be one of them.

”You will also send sky-writer, the plane-thingy,” he gestured out at the yellow-orange morning sky, “To say ‘Shane Hollander loves Ilya Rozanov.’”

“I think that might be a little much, but, um.”

“But,” Ilya prompted.

“We could, I don’t know, let them guess? And then maybe, someday, if it's like, safe or whatever and you wanted to confir—“

With a wounded sound, Ilya shoved himself upright.

His phone—the same phone he’d had five years ago, now cracked and scuffed and held together by a fraying case and similarly broken screen protector—was being shoved into his face. He recognized the flag with its red maple leaf, obviously, but the little logo on the top of the email said IRCC. “I am Canadian resident,” he said. “Brand new, just approved. I will pick up paperwork from post office when we get home.”

“What the—How are you just telling me this?”

“It is new. I did not have lawyer or paperwork until two months ago, but team and lawyers and Canada think I am national treasure so,” Ilya pursed his lips.

Shane leaned forward and kissed him, just a little peck on the lips, nothing particularly scandalous, but it cut through the charmingly arrogant pout he’d been trying to give Shane and melted it into something more genuine, like he was startled by a desire to laugh or smile for real.

“You are serious,” Ilya said, not quite asking.

“I am. About this.” He felt the shift. It was easier to follow the flow of the conversation with Ilya. Maybe it was because they were so close with one another now, or because Ilya had always been Shane's person. “Is,” he squeezed the hand he was still holding, “Is that something you’d want, too?”

“Is this something I—“ a rapid, beautiful, entirely incomprehensible to Shane flurry of Russian cut off Ilya’s English. Shane really needed to download an app or get a tutor or something. “Of course I want,” he finally continued in English. “I have been pinching myself since we started this conversation,” he shifted to show Shane the irritated skin on his arm, red with two white epicenters from where he had indeed been squeezing the skin. “I am still not convinced I am awake. I might wake up in bed and this will all be a dream and I—” He cut himself off, making a sound almost like a whine, something wordless, pure emotion.

“It’s not,” Shane said. “I want to be with you. I want to play with you.” He tugged Ilya closer. “I want to win with you.”

”You will have to tell me this again, I think,” Ilya said when they’d resettled, his head on Shane’s chest, ear pressed to his heart.

He liked that position, Shane had come to realize, these last few weeks. For a moment, he felt a horrible pang of longing for all the time they'd wasted pretending, those four horrible, lost years.

“As many times as you want.”

There were other questions—feasibility, the organization, all the other things that had a low-level anxiety skittering in the back of Shane’s skull—but they could wait. This was more important. He knew it would take work to fix what he had broken, even if it wasn’t entirely his fault. He wanted to do that work, lay down the framework of what would hopefully be the rest of their lives together. He’d do whatever it took to make sure Ilya knew he was serious, because he was.

Chapter 4

Chapter Notes

Today on Hollanov speedruns their long overdue relationship: the meet the parents episode.

(Also, yes, chapter count went up. I'm partway through chapter 12 and it's only October in-universe. Oops?)

June, 2021
Ottawa, ON


Shane did not like driving Ilya’s sports cars, no matter how often Ilya told him he could just take one of his until he had a contract and was ready to buy his own. Even this one, which Ilya insisted was his slowest, most boring vehicle, not to mention his only SUV, made Shane feel a little bit uncomfortable. Still, it was worth it to pull up at the arrivals in the Mercedes (rather than a rental, or worse, his mom’s jeep) to see Ilya’s face shift with surprise. A part of him had recoiled at Ilya walking through the parking lot after his long flight from Las Vegas, even though he knew Ilya flew all the time and often drove himself.

“I thought you might chicken out,” Ilya called into the open rear hatch as he slung his backpack and carry-on into the trunk. He was pretty incognito in his backward baseball cap and athletic clothes, though Shane would recognize the shape of him anywhere. “I would have gone to parking lot.”

“Hello to you, too,” Shane replied, looking at Ilya in the rearview. They shared a grin as Ilya slammed the trunk shut rather than using the perfectly functional button that did it automatically, then he was sliding into the passenger seat, giving Shane a quick peck on the cheek as he pulled the door closed behind him. He laughed when Shane didn’t pull away, thrilled.

”You missed me,” he sing-songed, buckling his seatbelt before Shane could instruct him to. Only then did Shane put the car in drive.

“Yeah,” Shane allowed, adding, “You missed me too.” He navigated around the wayward throngs of Canadians waiting to pick up their travelers with ease.

Ilya’s gaze burned into the side of his head. He hadn’t denied it.

Shane turned to look at him for only a blink. He was driving, after all. “What?”

“You look good driving my car,” Ilya said smugly.

“And?”

”You missed me,” Ilya drawled teasingly, reaching over the center console for his hand.

”We did just establish that,” Shane groused, but laced their fingers together. “How were the MLH awards?”

Ilya relaxed into his seat, ripping off his hat and throwing it carelessly into the back seat. “Boring. Hunter gave a whole speech about being a gay icon—“

”He was pretty brave,” Shane remarked.

“Yes, yes, but you did the hard part for him, moya lyubov. You were the first. I did give my congratulations to Kip, though. Hunter actually brought him.”

Looking over the edge of his sunglasses, Shane turned to Ilya. The vehicle rolled smoothly toward a red light. ”Told you he would.”

“Bah. Kip deserves better.”

Shane flicked his blinker on and coasted through the turning lane up to the line. “Did you talk to him at all?”

“Hunter?” Ilya sighed. “Yes,” he slurred the word like a hiss. “Club nearby had Scott Hunter Night. He was there, looking scared half to death but also very happy. We talked, I told him being gay was okay, he looked at me funny, I danced a little but he still didn’t get the idea, so I left.”

“You probably could have told him.”

“I have done everything but told him, Shane. You cannot fix stupidity, yes?”

Shane’s gaze flitted over for a second. ”I guess.”

They made it most of the way back to Ilya’s before he spoke again. “I do not know that I will ever have love for him, bravery or not. I do not like how he turned such a blind eye.”

”He really didn’t know about what they did.”

“Obviously I don’t want him to stop playing unless his arthritic bones make decision for him,” Ilya explained indelicately, “But is not about on ice, is about off it. Is bad look for him to be top liaison between players and league. He was Crowell’s guy, so he should resign from PA job.”

“I mean, maybe, but, Crowell went on the record and called Kip the f-word, and Scott denounced him. Plus the whole Lily leak was kind of a doublecross. He helped.”

”You are not going to get me to believe our getting back together is fucking Scott Hunter’s fault,” Ilya insisted. “Has nothing to do with him.”

“No, I know. He just started the conversation. Kind of.”

Ilya looked outraged. “You did that. Was your plan. Not him.”

”Okay, okay. Scott did nothing,” Shane relented. “But by that logic, this is all on you.”

”Someone must bear the burden,” he returned loftily.

Shaking his head, Shane said, “Way to be humble about it, asshole.”


—


In a perfect world, Shane would have driven them straight to his cottage, stopping maybe once to get Ilya something from Tim Hortons. They would spend the next month wrapped up in one another, removed from the outside world, until it was time to emerge, relaxed and sated and ready to begin training. He would speak only to family and friends, a small, hand-chosen group of people he loved and tune out everything and everyone else.

The thing was that this was not a perfect world. It would never be. Shane could only control so much, something he still struggled with pretty regularly. This summer was more of a stepping stone, the foundation of something better, a way to futureproof summers where they could stay tangled up in one another for longer than the twoish weeks he hoped they would have between now and the end of August.

So instead of hoofing it two hours to his cottage, Shane drove them back to Ilya’s place and that led them to this: a quick romp in Ilya’s bedsheets, a too-short afternoon nap, followed by Ilya trying on three different shirts while he thought Shane was distracted reading emails from his agent. Naturally, Shane got caught ogling him and Ilya went on the defensive.

“What? I want to look good for your parents.”

Shane smiled. “You always look good. Besides, I think they’ll be surprised no matter what.”

“Oh, you think?”

He felt his smile widen. “Yeah.”

Ilya studied the reflection of himself in his closet mirror. “I could get one of my old Raiders shirts, I know you said your mom loves the Raiders these days.”

“I said she hates the Metros.”

He straightened his black t-shirt. The sleeves hugged his arms, drawing attention to them. “Mm, same thing, yes?”

“I love you,” Shane said.

It never failed to catch Ilya off guard. He flinched, just a little bit, whirling around to look Shane in the eye, his own darting around, trying to figure out what it was that had prompted Shane to say the words.

“What did I do?” He asked, when he couldn’t figure it out.

Shrugging, Shane said, “Nothing. I just wanted to say it.”

Because really, it wasn’t like he could put it into words anyway. He’d never been any good at that sort of thing.

Ilya’s pursed lips and arrogant smile lessened from something practiced and pouty to sweet and gentle. The lack of confidence in his expression made him look younger, boyish and disarmed.

“I love you, too, Hollander,” he said, blinking at him, a little wide eyed. He said something else in Russian, which was beautiful and unfair and a little harsh in the way his native tongue always was.

“What was that?” Shane asked.

“Nothing.”

“You’ll have to teach me Russian someday.”

“Maybe.”


—


His parents' house looked imposing from the road, perfectly kept thanks to his dad keeping up on things between bi-weekly visits from the lawn care company. His mom's domain was inside, a combination of perfectly organized chaos in family areas, while the spaces more likely to host guests remained picturesque.

“Very Canadian,” Ilya commented from the passenger seat of his own vehicle. Somehow Shane had landed himself the role of chauffeur, which he was choosing to believe was because Ilya liked to be treated like royalty and not because he’d shaken his head once when Shane had said they could take whichever too-fast car Ilya wanted.

“If the shoe fits.”

“I think it does,” Ilya said. “Like glove. That is the saying, right?”

Shane smiled at him as he unbuckled his seatbelt. He could see his mom’s silhouette in the large bay window and wondered just how long she’d been pacing, if that would make it better or worse. Ilya wasn’t the only nervous person in the car. “It’s going to be okay.”

“Are you telling me or yourself?”

“Both of us, I think.”

Ilya snorted, then leaned over and kissed him. “I will be on my best behavior.”

“Somehow I doubt that,” he said, smile widening. “Come on, before she comes out here.”

His mom had been on him since the day he’d authorized Scott Hunter to share the name, prying in the slightly passive aggressive way she did when someone was holding out on her. It wasn’t mean, but it was targeted. She didn't like knowing only the peripheral details of his life, especially when he'd asked for her help. He understood, but he hadn’t wanted to share anything until he was sure that Ilya wanted to be together.

So when he’d finally told his parents he wanted them to meet Lily at long last, his dad had nodded and his mom had said fucking finally under her breath before giving him the options of going out to dinner or having something more casual at home.

Shane had opted for casual. It was only June thirtieth, and while he’d be a free agent at midnight, he wanted to forestall the craziness. Farah would call him first thing in the morning with the first round of offers and her recommendations which usually included a twenty-four hour waiting period.

He slipped out of the car and tucked the key in his pocket, coming around the front end to grab Ilya’s hand.

“You nervous?”

“Terrified,” Ilya admitted, wincing. “You are sure they will like me?”

“So sure,” Shane confirmed. “Come on.”

He dragged Ilya up the walk and inside. His mom stood just inside the door waiting for them to kick their shoes off. His dad emerged from the kitchen, dish towel thrown over his shoulder dotted with a bit of pasta sauce. The house smelled like basil and oregano and tomato, the day long simmer of sauce that preceded his dad’s incredible chicken parmesan.

“Hi Mom, Dad,” he started, careful to keep Ilya’s hand locked in his. With his free hand, he gestured to Ilya, who rested at his side in a line of tension. “This is Ilya. Rozanov. But you already know that.”

“Hi,” Ilya said, voice a little too low to be casual.

“My boyfriend,” Shane added, just to make sure it was clear.

His dad moved first.

“Hi,” Dad said, reaching around Mom to shake Ilya’s hand. “I’m David. Hope you like chicken parm.”

“I do,” Ilya said stiffly.

“Dad’s is the best,” Shane said. “He pulled out all the stops.”

“Your mom made the garlic bread,” his dad informed them. “It’s really good.”

All eyes went to his mom, who hadn’t moved. She was looking at Ilya very intently, studying him like she could see into his skull, reading every thought in his brain. Shane felt the fine tremor in Ilya’s hand as he waited for her judgment.

“Hello, Ilya,” she said finally. Shane watched in alarm as she swiped at her eyes and her chin wobbled slightly. “Sorry, I um,” she exhaled and shook her head and said, through tears, “Please, come in.”

Ilya stayed where he was as the rest of them moved further inside, the tension in their joined hands keeping Shane from taking more than a pair of steps away from him. “I do not want to make your mother uncomfortable,” he murmured, unaware that Yuna Hollander could hear a pin drop from across the street.

“Oh, sweetheart,” Mom turned back to them, once again swiping away an errant tear. “It’s not you,” she said. “I just,” she sniffed. “You were in Boston, weren’t you? When Hayden and I talked.”

Ilya's voice was tight. Small. “No. He came to see me in Ottawa. Marleau, too.”

Shane closed his eyes, squeezed them shut as tight as he could. It was good for him to be reminded of the cost of his actions, the harm he’d caused trying to do everything the right way. What good was finesse if he still hurt the people he cared about? Just because Ilya had forgiven him, it didn’t mean Shane forgave himself. He wasn’t sure he ever would.

When he finally opened his eyes, his mom was standing before Ilya, going up on her tiptoes to wrap her arms around his neck.

What Ilya didn’t know, even as he wrapped his arms around her carefully, was that this wasn’t the kind of thing his mom did. She wasn’t a hugger. Shane was raised in a perfectly normal household, where he and his parents said I love you often and spoke more days than they didn’t. But his mom had never been particularly tactile with anyone who wasn’t Shane’s dad. Sure, they hugged when they said hello or goodbye before and after flights or when a conversation took a lot out of them, but that was specifically for their family unit. It wasn’t the kind of thing Shane expected her to extend to his boyfriend. Not right away, at least.

She said something to Ilya, the words muffled by Ilya’s face, her lips too difficult to read through the curtain of her hair.

Ilya nodded along with whatever she said and she squeezed him a little bit tighter in response.

“Okay,” she decided after a moment, which Ilya correctly interpreted as his cue to step back. She looked between the two of them, then embraced Shane, planting a brief kiss on his forehead. “Okay,” she repeated, mostly to console herself. “We are all going to dry our eyes and enjoy David’s very delicious cooking.”

“I’m not crying,” Shane pointed out.

“Must be nice,” Ilya replied, sniffing.

“Must be,” his mom agreed.

There was no more crying at dinner, and not because his dad made a comment about having put enough salt in the sauce. There had been a few close calls during what Shane thought of as his parents’ interrogation, the long overdue questioning he’d warned Ilya was coming. Mostly, it was Ilya, in his carefully spoken English, saying things like I have only ever loved one person, and Summer before rookie season, I think, but probably since always, while Shane nudged his foot under the table and inarticulately said things like only one and me too.

Ilya ate more than Shane had ever seen, asked his mom how the bread was so crusty and tasty as he mopped up every last drop of sauce with it and they almost—almost made it through the entire night without anyone bringing up the countdown to free agency.

“So,” Mom said, when they were sitting at the cleared kitchen table, the dinner wine switched for vodka that had made Ilya’s eyes light up with surprise when his dad had pulled it out of the refrigerator rather than the freezer—the correct way to store it, he’d explained to Shane. “You’re signing with Ottawa?”

Shane groaned. “Mom,” he started.

“Is good team,” Ilya said. “Better every year.”

“Shane was a Centaurs fan as a kid,” his dad chimed in, only to react with a silent, telegraphed, What? when his mom gave him what Shane could only refer to as The Look.

“You said you wanted to come home,” she said. “I thought Canada. Ottawa is still years away from a cup.”

Ilya made a sound that suggested he had a very different opinion on the subject.

“I don’t want to do long distance again,” Shane said plainly. “I know we weren’t really in a relationship before, but it sucked.”

“And you’ll both be out? Russia’s going to be okay with that?”

“Mom!”

Ilya set his vodka down, shoved his thigh a little closer to Shane’s, the warmth of his body bleeding through his jeans. “No,” he said with a shrug, “Of course not. But I applied for permanent residency when I learned Shane still had feelings for, ah, Lily.” Shane tapped the toe of his shoe against Ilya’s. Ilya tapped back harder. “My Russian passport is good for long enough to get citizenship in Canada. Is not perfect, but I have lawyers. Team does not know about me yet, but they know I want Canadian citizenship. They were good to Briggs, and we have other queer people on staff. They will protect me if it comes to that.”

“He’s going to apply for citizenship once he hits the residency requirement,” Shane said.

“And that’s what you want?” His mom asked.

Shane lifted his chin to answer, but he wasn't the one being asked.

Beside him, Ilya took Shane’s hand before it could drum anxiously against his knee and placed it on his thigh instead. “Yes,” he said, nodding once, sharp and confident. “I will never go back to Russia. It is sad, but,” he pursed his lips, “It is nothing compared to the sadness I felt without Shane in my life these last few years.”

“Fuck you,” Shane said, feeling his eyes begin to well up.

Ilya tilted his head, looking so much like he wanted to say later, but he wouldn’t dare with his parents on the other side of the table. Instead, he said I love you in Russian, those three words that had quickly become Shane’s favorite Russian phrase, while his parents tittered quietly to themselves, thoroughly convinced that the love they saw on the other side of the table was genuine.

Chapter 5

Chapter Notes

This chapter brought to you by free agency, a small spot of canonical angst, and cute couple things.

July, 2021
Ottawa, ON


Ilya was like an overexcited puppy once July hit, signalling the beginning of free agency. He was manic to an extent that Shane wondered if he'd slept the night before, if a sudden crash was incoming. The lack of walls between them—or maybe Shane’s desire for there not to be walls—made him giddy and sweet. He liked when Shane held his finger up to his lips in a demand for silence while he spoke to his agent, Farah Jalali, his eyes flashing in interest and focus when Shane put the call on speaker so they could listen to her recount the list of yesterday’s offerings together. It had been a slower than usual first day of free agency, the market holding its breath to see where Shane would land before teams went through the usual motions.

“Vancouver is all in,” she told him. “Edmonton, too. Both said some iteration of the sky being the limit, which means they'll go up to sixteen if you push. Calgary came in with fourteen, but said they could go fifteen five if you sign an eight year deal. In the States—” there was the sound of aggressive clicking as Farah rapidly went through emails and spreadsheets, confirming her data.

Ilya slithered down the bed, sleep warm and rumpled despite his earlier enthusiasm. He rested his head in Shane’s lap, arms coming around Shane’s middle, nose pressed to Shane's belly. He looked up intently, though his eyes closed when Shane dug his fingers into Ilya's tangled curls to scratch his scalp.

“Honestly, Shane, I've got offers from twenty eight of thirty two teams. I'd consider maybe fifteen of those.”

Ilya pinched his side, but Shane was pretty sure they had the same questions. “Who didn't bother?” He paused. “Beside the obvious.”

“Winnipeg, but I expected that. They have maybe six million in cap space and a couple guys up for extension. They did send a nice message saying they wished they could afford you.” Farah hummed. “Saint Louis offered five, because it's what they have, so I took them off the board. Thanks for playing.”

“And the Southwest?”

“Nope, they're in. Vegas offered a blank check and LA is desperate—they’d fire Lankeshaw into the sun if you said yes, but we agreed they were a no.” Shane could hear the frown in her voice. “Ottawa took Winnipeg's approach. Their GM called me to say they'd love to bring a hometown boy home but they didn't want to send an insulting offer. Rozanov's contract is up in two years and they're all in on him and Haas. Hayes is going to need a significant bump to consider sticking around after Toronto short changed him on his last deal and the number sounds like four and a half, which is a deal if you ask me.” She paused, noting, “Briggs is up next summer, too. Ottawa is loyal to their guys, I'll give them that.” Another pause. “They offered their facilities for the off-season, though. No questions asked. Kind of them.”

“They've been very supportive,” Shane agreed, feeling Ilya breathe out against his abdomen. He could hear the business-like tone of his own voice.

Ilya was watching him again. Wondering, probably, how Shane would play his hand.

“Thank everyone in the Southeast and West—who isn't Canadian,” he clarified, “For their interest. If you get asked, narrow it down to the Canadian teams, Boston, New York—”

“I'm sure that won't be taken out of context,” Farah pointed out.

“Probably will, but that's the new reality, right?”

“God, I love you Shane. You are my favorite client.”

Shane felt himself flush right around the same time Ilya muffled his snort of laughter into Shane's thigh, “Let’s add Detroit. I'm sure that will generate some buzz about me and Rose, but she knows it's coming. She checked in with me yesterday.”

Ilya went still against him.

Fuck, Shane thought. They'd talked about a lot of things over the last few weeks, but they’d never broached the subject of Rose.

Of course she was a sore spot. Had he even told Ilya that they were friends? He didn’t think it had come up. She'd been abroad filming the latest X-Squad movie for months and even when she wasn’t on location they had always talked sporadically. He kept her updated, even though he was pretty sure she was the most online person he’d ever met, but talking about her hadn't really registered to Shane as being important because for him, it was so far in the past.

That didn't mean Ilya felt the same way, he realized. He really sucked at this boyfriend thing, didn't he?

“Sounds good,” Farah said, pulling him out of the tailspin his stupid mouth had sent him into. “Do you have an idea of where you want to land? I know we agreed to table so you could think, but I would like to know what the short list is.”

“Ottawa,” he said. “Let the other teams talk. I'm sorry, I know that's more work for you, but—”

“To be clear,” she said. “Just Ottawa?”

“Yeah.”

There was a brief pause, the sound of a keyboard chittering quietly as Farah took notes. “Shane, Ottawa is out,” she told him, an edge in her voice that sounded a lot like she was talking to a child who couldn’t be bothered to listen, “They literally cannot afford you.”

“They can if I give them a deal.”

“Shane, if this is what you want—”

“I want to come home.” The words were a refrain. He'd said them so many times, but they were true, so he kept saying them. “My family—” he tightened his grip in Ilya's hair, made sure he was looking directly into Ilya's sad, serious eyes, not at his chin or cheeks or the line of his stubbled jaw, “Is here.” Ilya swallowed and looked away. “I want to play here,” he finished. “ I’ll sign for eight. For anyone else, the floor is fifteen, but,” he sighed, hoping that would convey he wouldn't be taking it. “The league’s paying me fifty million in damages. It's not like I'm struggling over here.”

“Okay,” Farah placated, sensing he was serious. “Okay. I would remind you that you paid almost half of that in lawyer fees, but I can tell your mind’s made up.” She paused, but the sound of activity on her end suggested she was making notes. “Ottawa it is. I'll put out feelers. They’ll probably need time to get Rozanov on board, even though I'm willing to bet you covered that on your trip to Boston last month as part of some well formed plan you strategically failed to mention…”

Wow, Ilya mouthed. She's good.

“Guilty,” he admitted. “I could call Carlton and make the offer myself—”

“Shane, you pay me literal millions to represent you. Please let me be your agent. I’ll call Carlton, tell him you’re interested and see what he’s willing to offer.”

And Ilya was back to laughing against him, though it seemed to be startled out of him against his will. Shane smacked his shoulder, which made him smirk and squeeze Shane around the middle before detangling himself and pointing toward the shower.

Shane grabbed his wrist to stop him. Unfortunately for Ilya, he'd guessed correctly: Farah was very, very good at what she did, do Shane was expecting what came next.

“Have you talked to Lily about what happens next?” She paused. “I assume that's the reason for all the background noise.”
