Ilya whipped around, then perched himself beside Shane on the bed, throwing his hands up as if to indicate Farah must have very good hearing because he'd been very quiet the entire time.

“Hey, you called it,” Shane deadpanned. “She's the best in the game.” To Farah, he added, “We've discussed it. I don't plan on making a statement—” he laughed at himself a little, “And before you say anything, I know, I’m surprised at me too,” he gave Ilya a helpless look, “People will find out when they find out.”

“It might be sooner than you think, especially if Pike posts more pictures of Mister Rozanov looking at you with heart eyes on Instagram.”

“What the fuck—” Shane shoved a hand over Ilya’s mouth.

He was certain Farah had heard Ilya and his very distinct voice, but went with a flat, “I have no idea what you're talking about.”

“You did not just use your media voice on me,” Farah exhaled, blowing out the phone speaker for a second. “I officially retract my earlier statement about you being my favorite client. I can already feel the headache coming on,” She said it tiredly, but her next words were accompanied by a rare warmth. “Good for you both.”

“Thanks, Farah.”

Ilya looked over at him for permission. Shane nodded. A little hesitant, Ilya added a quiet, “Thank you.”

“Let me know if you or your agent need any backup,” Farah told Ilya, then it was back to business.

Ilya went tense and still as Shane threw around terms (as long as possible, full no move clause) and Farah indicated they were both a given, followed by a heads up that they would have to discuss his relationship at some point, especially once the contract went through. She needed answers if she was going to be able handle matters on his behalf.

Honestly, Shane would just read the summary she emailed him later. The Ottawa situation would sort itself out eventually. Interest was there and that was enough to get the ball rolling. There were more important things

“So,” he ventured, “About Rose—”

“It is done then,” Ilya spoke over him, a little too fast for Shane to believe it wasn't premeditated. “As good as, right?” He paused. When Shane looked at him a little too expectantly, he switched topics. “Maybe I should fire my agent and work with Farah. Would you mind? She is very good.”

“Ilya.”

“What?”

His voice was so much harsher in the morning, each consonant practically weaponized by his accent. Shane wanted to burrow his face against Ilya's neck and nuzzle until the stubble there started to chafe his cheeks. Shane turned to him, tilting his head. “We're friends. We talk when she's not on set and I'm not too deep into the season. It's not—” he narrowed his gaze at the all too familiar look on Ilya's face, watching him try to play down his emotions. “I'm gay, Ilya.”

“I know that, Shane.” He replied petulantly. “But not so gay you couldn't fuck Rose Landry.”

“Twice,” he said, even though it was like one and a half times, maybe, “And both times were total disasters.” He'd never confessed that to anyone before. His cheeks felt hot.

“Disasters like how?”

Ilya could definitely see his blush.

“Let’s just say she isn’t looking for a repeat performance,” he groused. “It was years ago. I was scared. I thought I could pretend and I couldn't.” He scratched at a bit of fuzz stuck to Ilya's face to remove it, watching the way that handsome, serious face went slack as he processed Shane's words. “She figured it out and we agreed to stay friends.”

“Figured it out like she told you?” The look on Ilya’s face was thunderous, like he didn’t think any of this was particularly funny.

“I'm not exactly known for being good at like, relationships,” Shane pointed out. It wasn't a direct answer, but it wasn't a lie. He wasn’t ashamed of how it played out between them—he’d gained something far more valuable out of the demise of their relationship than he had by trying to force it—but he didn’t want to rehash crying into his drink at Ciel while Rose tried to hook him up with Miles and his heart tried to tear itself into itty bitty pieces that would take years to put back together.

“You are doing fine,” Ilya said.

Shane huffed. “Easy for you to say, you’re really good at,” he gestured between them, which Ilya shook his head at, “All this. I don’t—You don’t have to worry about Rose. I’ll introduce you over like, facetime or something. She doesn't know about you yet—nobody knew until Hayden and Cliff figured it out, but she knew there was someone. I was pretty obvious.”

“Fine,” he relented, placated for now, “But you are wrong. More people know than that.”

“What?” It felt like someone dumped a bucket of cold water over Shane’s head. “Who?”

“Svetlana, Hazy, and my rookies.” Ilya looked at him like he was crazy, which meant Shane hadn't done a good job of hiding his surprise about that second part. “You told Briggsy, yes?”

“I didn’t.”

“Ah. Then Luca figured it out. He is very smart, that one.” He smiled fondly. “You will like him. Like mini Shane Hollander but more top energy.”

“Oh my god, Ilya,” Shane drawled, letting his head drop sideways. It thunked against Ilya's cheek before dropping to his shoulder. “You can't just like… out people.”

“I know Derek told you he is with Luca. I bet he tells you everything. I know what your name is in his phone.”

Shane flushed. “Yeah. I mean, kind of, but still.”

“So? What is big deal? I know, you know, is not like we are screaming it from rooftops to strangers.”

“You’re unbelievable.” Shane rose, throwing his phone behind him on the bed as he pushed past Ilya to take the ensuite. “See if I let you shower with me now.”

“Mm, you will,” purred Ilya. “You know I will make it worth your while.”

He wasn’t wrong, but Shane still made a whole production out of pretending to shut him out.


—


The messages came at a breakneck pace. Ilya was busy packing a bag to take to the cottage, but he'd left his phone behind to annoy Shane in the living room.

“I think your team chat is blowing up,” Shane yelled across the house, right around the time the phone calls and facetime requests started.

“Well, someone waited a week to sign the contract they'd practically written themselves,” Ilya called from what he'd begun referring to as their bedroom, which made Shane smile every single time, “Then went full primadonna about press access and embargos.”

“Sorry I didn't want to blow up our summer with a bunch of media appearances,” he replied, hearing the moody edge of his own voice. “I was hoping we'd be at the cottage when the news broke.”

“And whose fault is that?”

It was Ilya's and they both knew it, so Shane said, “You're the one who spent three hours deciding what color iPhones we needed from the Apple Store—”

He tried and failed to wipe the smile off his face before Ilya popped his head out of the bedroom, yelling, “I am sorry, I wanted to match with my boyfriend who wanted to be very boring and pick black when I know he liked the purple one better—”

Shane spoke over him, “Then insisted on going into the store to make sure everything transferred over even though I'd paid to have said phones—whose color I compromised on, you asshole—delivered.”

“I did not want to lose any pictures!”

Shane shook his head. “Your boyfriend said he could help you with that at his cottage—”

“Yes but if something happened because my phone is as old and decrepit as Scott Hunter my boyfriend would never forgive himself.”

Ilya was right about that, at least. Ilya also knew that he was right, which made him very smug as he sauntered into the living room and reached for one of the two red phones on the table.

Naturally, he picked up Shane's, which he only knew because the face ID didn't work. He turned it toward Shane but Shane snatched it from his hands and handed the very annoyingly buzzy one to him instead, which connected immediately when it saw his face blinking down at the screen.

“Rozanov! Finally!” A loud voice called, testing the new speaker “I know you know the news. Monk’s. Now.”

“Bood, I am busy packing for trip,” He argued.

“I’ve seen you pack in four minutes, Cap. This is way more important and you know it. Shane fucking Hollander signed. Captain Canada is going to play for our goddamn team, and I want to know who the fuck is responsible for it.”

“Embargo lifts at six,” Ilya looked to him for confirmation, even though Shane was about ninety nine percent positive he knew exactly what time everything was set to occur.

“It’s four thirty. Fucking happy hour, man. Just come out. I asked Briggsy to get a hold of Hollander but he hasn't answered yet. I think he went golfing or some shit with the rest of the kids.”

“I'm leaving in a couple hours,” he groaned, looking to Shane for backup.

“Russia can wait, Roz,” Bood insisted incorrectly, almost whining. “This is important.”

Shane shrugged.

Ilya's eyes darkened. “On second thought, maybe. I will call you back.”

He hung up while Zane Boodram was still talking.

“Now you don't want to leave?”

“We can meet Boodram for happy hour.”

“You do not understand.”Ilya threw himself across the couch, invading Shane's personal space. “Boodram is inviting the entire team. Is what he does. He is, what the fuck is—ah! Master of ceremonies. And grill. He is very serious about barbecue. You will see.” He cut himself off. “It will become massive party. If we go we will not make it to the cottage until tomorrow afternoon.”

Shane wanted to go to the cottage. He wanted it so badly, but the part of his brain hardwired to being a professional knew it was important to bond with his new teammates.

“Yeah, but they're technically my teammates now. We could surprise them. Plus, I haven't seen Derek yet, he went with Luca and their friends to Cabo, so.”

“Yes,” Ilya hummed, “The kids trip. I saw all the pictures. I am glad they were sloppy in the group chat and not on Instagram.

Shane smirked. He'd gotten more than a couple drunk texts from Derek. It was good that he'd felt comfortable letting loose a little.

“They will shit themselves if you come with me.” Ilya was definitely scrolling through said group chat now. “They think you fucked off to your fortress of solitude in the wilderness of Lanaudière.”

Shane rolled his eyes. “Isn't that the ice palace thing from Superman?”

“I don't know, probably? Fucking Hazy and his superhero shit,” Ilya swore. His eyes flicked up over the edge of his phone. “You are sure you want to go?”

“Yeah.” He watched Ilya's smile unfurl. “Do you think we need to tell Derek and Luca to keep things quiet?”

“I think they know better, but we will call them on the way.”

Chapter 6: Interlude: Derek

Chapter Notes

Double update this week. Monks today, Cottage tomorrow. 😎

Derek probably would have ignored group chat blowing up if Luca hadn’t made a sound like a mouse, sharp and shrill and way too fucking cute to ignore. They were on their final hole of the afternoon—there was a joke in there somewhere but Derek liked to think he was mature enough not to make it—Bergy at the tee box about to swing.

“You alright over there, Haas?”

“No,” Luca told them on a delay, eyes never leaving his phone. “I mean, I’m fine, but holy fuck.”

“What? What’s going on?”

“We signed Hollander.”

Bergy dropped his club, his ball rolling off the box and down the slope. That was fair, because Derek’s jaw was currently making a valiant attempt at hitting the ground.

“No fucking way,” LaPointe said. “We can’t afford Captain Canada. Even you said that, Derek.”

“I did,” Derek replied, though nobody had listened when he said Shane didn’t like being called that. “Who reported it?”

“No reports. It’s in team chat. Apparently they’re dropping the presser tonight and wanted to give us the heads up in advance,” Young moved fast when he was motivated, and for a man who acted like he was more brawn than brains on the ice, his reading speed was rapid. “Hazy just posted a meme of the bat signal with a twenty-four instead of a bat, and Bood’s calling for everybody to get to Monk’s asap.”

“And they want you to call him,” Luca informed Derek, explaining, “Since you’re the only person on the team with his number.”

Derek wasn’t so sure about that. He was pretty sure their captain also had his number, a theory Luca also agreed with, despite his insistence otherwise to their friends. “Let’s finish up here and I’ll text him. We can call it now or—”

“Uh, yeah, we’re calling it,” Pointer was looking at him like he’d grown another head, Bergy nodding profusely beside him, “My ass will be on a barstool to watch the broadcast of that presser because I cannot believe it.”

“Hear, hear,” Holmberg agreed. “Everybody to the cart. I’m driving.”

Bergy, as the only sports car owner and enthusiast among them, liked to be the one driving in critical situations. Personally, Derek loved chirping him for it. It was hilarious.

“You know it’s a golf cart, right? This thing might go thirty miles an hour with a half ton of hockey player and gear loaded on it.”

“Shut up, Bigrig, or you’ll be walking back to the clubhouse.”


—


Monk's was dead, as it usually was in the off-season. An alt rock song blasted a little too loudly over the speakers and the TVs showed highlights from a Blue Jays game, closed captions fucked up and perpetually behind. Derek had played baseball as a kid, but much like golf, he barely tolerated playing and downright hated watching it.

It was good, then, that Luca was tucked under his arm, Bergy, Pointer, and Young coming around them like heat-seeking projectiles, noticing the animated discussion between Bood and Hazy at the bar.

“Children!” Dykstra was also present, having just gotten a beer from their usual bartender, Laurie. “You look sun kissed. Keeping the good times rolling from Cabo?’

“Fuck me,” Luca said quietly, flushing hotly as he detached himself from Derek. “We're never going to live that down.”

“Probably not.” Definitely not, he thought.

There may have been a four am photo dump in the team group chat (rather than their friends chat) which included many, many incriminating photos of the five of them. Personally, Derek thought they were funny and cute, even if his hindbrain recoiled with a very specific kind of panic at not remembering the rave where most of the photos were taken. He certainly looked blackout drunk, eyes blown and glossy as he danced way too close with Luca for public consumption.

Roz had personally congratulated them for being young and stupid, which was a ringing endorsement and eternal damnation wrapped up into one.

So, no. They would not be living it down.

“Call your boy, Briggs,” Boodram ordered, patting the seat next to him. “Right fucking now.”

“Already tried. His phone is off.”

“Rozy told Bood there's gonna be a press conference.”

“Of course Roz knew,” Luca murmured. Wyatt elbowed him, shooting him a dry smile, which made Derek snort.

Of course Hazy knew everything, he thought.

To be fair, he wasn't the only one in the know. Derek and Luca had stopped home to drop their car off in anticipation of this turning into what their hangouts at Monk’s always turned into: a shit show that either closed the place down or sent them off to either Bood or Hazy's place to hang out and destroy five hundred bucks worth of pizza.

Thankfully, that also meant that it was just the two of them when Roz had called Luca to tell them to keep their mouths shut about anything they thought they knew, Shane nagging him in the background not to be an asshole and getting a half a greeting in before Roz said Smell you later, rookies and hung up while Shane told him off some more, which was about as close to verbal confirmation as they would get on something Luca and Derek had already convinced themselves they knew.

Luca had showed Derek the Instagram post that placed them in Boston with a couple of Raiders players courtesy of Hayden Pike. Derek hadn't been able to find his profile when he looked, but he never claimed to be savvy when it came to being online. At the time, Luca had given him a look that said he was kind of an idiot and said Of course he blocked you. He's jealous.

Of what, Derek didn't know. Pike was a damn good hockey player and most definitely Shane's best friend if those pictures were anything to go by, but whatever.

A couple of others showed up. Harris and Troy, who were together, no surprise, Harris looking tanned while Troy had a pink flush to his nose and cheeks that was definitely sunburn, came in looking gleeful. Bood immediately honed in on Harris, demanding details, because someone had to schedule important updates on the team's social media page and it was definitely him. Tanner Dillon showed up with Chewie not long after, the two of them waving at everyone on their way to the bartender, ordering drinks before joining the crowd.

Last—well, not last, technically—but not least was Rozanov, who threw open the door like he owned the place and yelled, “Listen up, Fuckers!” Like he was about to give a pregame speech rather than making a grand entrance at their local watering hole.

“Dude, we didn't buy out the bar,” Hazy yelled back.

Roz scanned the room, noticed the four-top in the corner with their half price nachos and pints and waved. They waved back. “They don't mind,” he decided on their behalf. “Now. I am sure you all have questions and want to watch big press conference. We will not be doing that.”

Tanner and Barrett booed him. Some of the other guys joined in. More than a couple ice cubes were fished from drinks and pelted at him.

Laurie, their favorite bartender, yelled, “Cut it out or get cut off, heathens,” which was why she was their favorite.

A chorus of apologies followed, but then it was back to business.

“Why no presser, Cap?” Bood asked.

“Finally someone asks,” Roz said, exasperated, acting like he'd been waiting forever. He reached behind himself to push the door open a crack. “We will not be watching because we have special guest.”

“No shot,” Pointer muttered somewhere behind Derek's back. “No fucking shot.”

Bergy and Young made similar sounds of anticipation and disbelief.

“Rozy, you're fucking killing us,” Hazy gestured to the gaggle of hockey players assembled at the bar. “Get on with it.”

“Fine, fine.” Roz sighed very dramatically. The door Roz hadn't cracked open swung wide as Shane stepped inside, pocketing his sunglasses and smiling a little awkwardly. Roz held his hands up like a conductor trying to silence the band before the big finale. “Everyone, allow me to introduce your new second favorite Centaur, Shane Hollander.”

“What the fuck, Rozanov,” Shane said, doing a poor job of looking upset. “Second favorite?”

“Yes, after me. Is my team.”

“Fuck you,” he said, shoving Roz, who looked positively incandescent at the prospect of banter between them. “This is my hometown.”

“Mmh, score more points than me and we can talk.”

“You know I will,” Shane glared up at him.

Roz grinned. “You will not.”

“Oh my god,” someone murmured. Derek thought it might have been Bergy. “We're gonna win the cup.”

Chouinard clapped Bood on the back, shoving him forward. They shared a little look as he did, raised eyebrows and shock giving way to excitement.

“Well? Are you fucks going to greet him or should we go back and tell Carlton you are not interested in playing with best players in hockey?”

“You shut your mouth, Rozy,” Bood strode forward and offered Shane his hand. “Holy shit,” he said, when they shook.

“Very eloquent, Mister Assistant Captain,” Roz swung around Bood, clapping his shoulder as he went over to the bar.

Shane smiled, a little lopsided. “Ignore him, he's very excited he got to do the grand reveal.”

“Sorry. I just can't fucking believe it. I know Carlton told us and Wiebe confirmed, but like, you're here.”

“For the next eight years,” he said.

“No fucking way.” Everyone looked over at Derek. “What? We're all fucking thinking it!”

Roz laughed at Derek’s expense as the bartender came over to serve him. With a put upon sigh, he said, “Laurie, tell me you have ginger ale. Canada's golden boy has returned home and I do not think it is on the menu.”

“I guess he has,” Laurie said, surveying the room. Her eyes were wide enough to betray her surprise, but like always, she was a professional. To be fair, it was her bar and had been the Cents’ local haunt for years. “I'll send one of the dishwashers to pick up a couple cases.”

“Thank you,” Roz said, and Derek didn’t need to see Laurie scoff to know he’d put way too much money on the bartop. Over his shoulder, he called, “You hear that, Hollander? Star treatment!”

“I can drink beer, you asshole,” Shane yelled back. The boys were lining up for handshakes and introductions. Outside of the rink it looked like a lopsided, pseudo-receiving line.

Roz rolled his eyes and gestured to the shelves of liquor behind the bar. “We are doing shots, Hollander,” He called back. “You will need chaser, da?”


—


They do a lot of shots. Did, Derek revised as Rozanov tried to summon everyone back to the bar for another. He definitely should not do any more. Everything was a little blurry, blame split between the drinks and shots and the dim light of the bar. He felt good. Luca sat across the table from him, talking right up in Pointer's face, hands flapping excitedly as he did.

He was so fucking adorable. And handsome. Derek wanted to do unspeakable things to him.

A glass of water landed solidly in front of him, ice cubes clinking. He watched over his shoulder as Shane dragged over a chair from an unoccupied table nearby to give himself a place to sit.

“I’m so glad you’re here,” Derek said, leaning over with the intention to hug his friend and mentor. Instead, he promptly lost his balance, nearly sliding out of his chair. He was such a klutz when drunk.

“Hey, kid—whoa.” Shane was a pillar, easily taking his weight to push him back upright. He laid a cold hand on the back of his neck while the room righted itself.

“Oh my God, Derek,” Luca giggled. “You're cut off.” He pointed in Derek's direction, but his finger was a little too far to the left.

Pointer hiccuped. “You're Shane Hollander.”

“That’s me,” he said. “You're,” he looked Pointer up and down. “LaPointe, right?”

“You know my name!” Derek was pretty sure he’d never seen Richard LaPointe look like an over-eager toddler before. He hoped he wasn’t too wasted to forget so he could give him shit for it in the group chat tomorrow.

“You introduced yourself to me,” Shane reminded Pointer, not unkindly. He hadn’t actually sat down yet, but he did make a slashy motion with his hand in the direction of the bar where Roz was hollering about them being lightweights. “I think all of you should probably be cut off. Waters?” He looked around the table.

“Dude, we can’t make Shane Hollander get us water,” Bergy whisper-shouted.

“You’re not making him do anything,” Derek advised, feeling a little bit like an expert on the subject. “He’s just like that.”

A hand patted Derek on the head, then proceeded to mess up his already messed up, likely very sweaty hair if the raucous laughter from Luca, Bergy, and Pointer were anything to go by. “Behave yourselves, children,” Shane said dryly.

“Ugh, stop it,” Derek was flushing as he tried and failed to make his hair sit the way it was supposed to.

Luca kicked him under the table when Shane walked away. At least, he was pretty sure it was Luca, because Bergy and Pointer were arguing about who won at golf earlier, despite both of them being tied before they called it quits.

“He’s hotter in person,” Luca complained, cheeks red with inebriation and embarrassment..

Derek rolled his eyes even though Luca wasn’t wrong. Shane had shown up in a linen shirt, shorts, and boat shoes, the usual summertime uniform of an athlete, except his shirt was unbuttoned half way and he wasn’t wearing an undershirt. As the night had gone on, even with the overhead fans turned up as fast as they could go and bar’s doors propped open—both entrances and the patio boasting signs that the place was closed after half the population of the city had tried to come celebrate with them—sweat had made his shirt stick to his frame, showing off sinew and muscle and… yeah. Derek wasn’t into Shane, but he could appreciate one of the most attractive men in the game.

“And so fuckin’ cool,” Young said, pouring himself into the seat Shane had dragged over. He had the drunken wobble going on, waving back and forth. “Y’gotta fight against the man, you know? Fuck the Metros. I’m glad they lost those picks.” He sighed, listing sideways against Derek and slurring, “I’m so fuckin’ hyped for camp.”

“Ooohkay,” Bood, who seemed to pop up out of nowhere, plucked the whiskey out of Young’s hand with the deftness of a parent, ignoring Young’s incoherent argument and flailing arms. “I think you kids are done.”

“Mom’s already getting us water,” Derek told him, which got everyone looking at him strangely. Well, almost everyone, because Luca was too busy laughing. “What? That’s what his name is in my phone!”

“I can feel the heat of that blush from here,” Bergy chirped. “You’re looking pink, Bigrig.”

“Fuck you!”

“Why do I feel like I’ve walked into something,” Shane remarked, handing Young a water before going around to the opposite side of the table to distribute the rest. He’d managed to convince one of the bartenders to give him a serving tray, which seemed about right.

Bood cackled. “Nothing too bad, Hollzy, Briggs was just telling us how you adopted him.”

“Oh,” Shane shrugged. “Yeah, more or less,” he said. “We lived together and he was kind of a mess.”

“Like how?” Pointer’s eyes had lit up. “He’s so stuffy and put together except when we get him shwasted.”

“You mean like now?” Bergy asked.

Derek felt his chest puff up. “What the hell do you mean, stuffy?”

He was too busy arguing that he was not stuffy, but rather far less immature than his younger teammates to notice Bood’s departure with the tray Shane had borrowed. He did notice when Shane pulled up another chair, this time in the gap between Bergy and Luca, the latter of which was chattering to him amicably, cheeks red with a flush that was definitely not all alcohol.

Shane had made a point to work the room. Over the course of the night he’d played darts and pool and even air hockey, then stood around and shot the shit with the boys while nursing his ginger ale. He wasn’t the most talkative guy, but it was different than Derek remembered. In Sweden, Shane came out when playoffs hit, or when it was mandatory—team dinners, championship celebrations, that sort of thing—he never showed up just because, and he certainly didn’t go around the room and make a point of talking to everyone. It was strange, kind of. He knew Shane was charismatic in that famous, yet down-to-earth sort of way. He just hadn’t expected to see what that looked like in a group social setting without it being an official function.

This was an impromptu get together, so either Shane had volunteered, or Rozanov had convinced him to come.

And speaking of Rozanov, he came by, vodka in hand, looking like he was fucking floating. Derek watched as he fist bumped all them, realized they were drinking water, and sighed.

“You are taking care of the kids already,” he said to Shane. “Why am I not surprised?”

Shane smirked up at him. “Probably last call for you too, Captain. Pull up a seat.”

“Oooh,” Bergy said, “He’s comin’ for Roz’s job.”

“Nonsense,” Roz handed Shane his drink to hold while he grabbed one of the few remaining chairs. He perched on it gracefully, crossing one leg over the other and leaning back like a rockstar, his shirt gaping open to reveal the glint of his necklace. Shane handed back the drink, eyes darkening as Roz took a sip of it and smacked his lips at the taste. “Hollander does not need letter on his chest to be leader, children. Is important lesson to learn. Actions matter and all that.”

“Who are you and where’s Roz?” Luca chirped, amiably.

Roz’s eyes narrowed. “You are lucky it is off season or I’d tell coach bag skate in the morning. I am always this wise, you are just not listening to me.”

“Okay, that’s bullshit,” Shane smacked Roz’s arm. “You called him a mini-me, so I know he’s listening carefully to everything his captain says.”

“Except for when he’s an asshole,” Luca revised.

Suck up, Derek thought, noting Shane’s smirk at his comment. He wondered if Luca would be so bold if he wasn’t drinking and decided he’d probably be a little mortified in the morning.

“It was nice knowing you, Haas,” Pointer gulped. “We should not have revisited that tequila.”

Laughter erupted from Roz. He shook his head, wiping tears from his eyes. “I love that my little rookie has claws. You really are baby Shane Hollander,” he told Luca, who couldn’t decide whether or not he was being trash talked or complimented so had chosen to purse his lips into a semi-petulant frown. Rozanov sobered, expression simmering into something rather serious for a chat with the team’s younger players twenty minutes before the bar closes. “But you will be better. Probably better than both of us,” he said, gesturing to Shane beside him. “You are so much smarter than we were at your age.”

His gaze briefly flicked to Derek halfway through the sentence. It felt like every nerve ending in Derek’s body lit up, but Roz was still talking.

“You are future of the franchise, Haasy. The next generation. We will build you a fucking empire and you will carry on our legacy.”

Luca had tears in his eyes when Derek managed to tear his eyes away from Rozanov. His lip was wobbling, but he set his shoulders and nodded like he was officially accepting some sort of mantle even though it was clear he didn’t trust himself to speak.

“I’ll drink to that,” Shane said, holding out his ginger ale to clink against their waters and Rozanov’s vodka.

Chapter 7

Chapter Notes

Today's chapter summary: 🏡⛈️🫂🚗🥺

Actual notes: Chapter count went up again. It will probably come down (or maybe not, I am but a conduit for my imagination and do not make the rules). I'm so sorry for those of you who were hoping for another ~30k word short story. I'm 55k words and 18.5 chapters in. I expect it to go another 20-30k.

PS: Thank you so much for sticking with me, I know waiting is the hardest part. I'd like these words to find their way from my brain and out through my fingers so I can share them with you faster. 💖

August, 2021
Lanaudière, QC


Ilya wandered out of the cool darkness of the bedroom looking rumpled and furious. It was late, nearly four in the afternoon. He’d been asleep since last night, forced awake by the shrill ring of his phone. Prior to that, he’d been semi-coherent, lucid just enough to grumble and groan at Shane when he checked in after his morning run to see if Ilya wanted to get up. He hadn’t, so Shane let him rest—he knew he’d be nothing but a nuisance if he went back to bed, incapable of holding still for very long with his brain wide awake and begging for stimulation—occupying himself with maintenance work around the cottage until storms rolled in and drove him inside.

The muffled Russian Shane could hear over the low drone of the tv had a sharp edge to it. Shane shuddered in the memory of the last time he’d heard that tone from a room over, but tried his best to pretend like he was focused on the documentary on screen.

He wasn’t sure if Ilya bought it, but it didn’t seem like he was up for talking. Instead, he perched himself on the edge of the couch, clad only in yesterday’s boxer briefs, before giving up and listing sideways to maneuver himself so that his head was in Shane’s lap and his body was curled up as small as he could make it.

Shane ignored the sour scent of morning breath and the tangible oily feeling beneath his fingers when they naturally found their way into Ilya’s hair.

Their first two weeks at Shane’s cottage had been utter bliss. A lot of it had been sex in every room, position, and manner that suited their fancy, but that was to be expected. When they untangled from each other enough to venture outside, they’d taken the kayaks out and swam in the lake, had bonfires, and even went up to his parents’ cabin for a night, getting drunk on his mom’s book club sangria. The next morning, his dad had made them his special hangover breakfast and Ilya declared it the best thing he’d ever eaten.

The last few days had been a little harder. Farah started pushing, asking Shane to commit to doing some media after his former coach Thieralt did a podcast circuit with some ‘tell-all’ story now that he was relieved of his duties. Ilya’s agent asked if he was going to blow off his brand deals after weeks of dodging calls and failing to disclose his newfound permanent residency to the person who had managed his career for the last twelve years. It was already starting to feel like the rest of the world was closing in. And then, this.

Whatever this was.

“You okay?” Shane asked, instead of do you want to talk about it? It seemed like the safer option, the one that wouldn’t make him defensive and angry right away. He kept his eyes on the wall above the TV, focused entirely on Ilya in a way that hopefully wouldn’t draw attention to it.

Ilya was silent for long moments, watching the yellow eyes of a panther emerge from the shadows of a rainforest on screen. The narrator droned on in a dry accent that sounded vaguely British. “Maybe,” he murmured, voice rough and sleepy. “I don’t know.” He sniffed. “I’m tired.”

The rain picked up outside, the trees rustling in the wind, casting shifting shadows across the hardwood. Raindrops pelted the roof in a muted staccato. Ilya blinked up at the skylights long enough to see where the new sounds had come from before smooshing his face against Shane’s thigh once more.

Shane pulled the soft throw blanket that was draped across the back of the couch over Ilya, who wiggled to cover himself up, pulling the coral colored linen up to his chin once he’d made sure the rest of him was cocooned sufficiently enough. He leaned down and kissed Ilya’s forehead once he’d settled, whispering a “You can go back to sleep if you want,” instead of pushing him to share if he didn’t want to.

A hand squeezed his knee, but Ilya remained quiet, tucked under the blanket and blinking sightlessly at the television.

They passed hours that way. It was dark—actual dark, not just from the storms—when Ilya forced himself upright. Shane had tried not to stare at him, but he was pretty sure Ilya had just laid there, somewhere between awake and sleep. Every time he’d looked down, Ilya’s eyes were half-lidded and empty, like someone had sucked all the life out of him.

“I will shower,” Ilya told him. “Then I have to go into town.”

“Want me to—”

“No.” The refusal felt like a slap, hard, and absolute. Ilya shook his head, his expression softening. “I have taken up enough of your day.”

That was a lie. Ilya had laid on Shane for a couple hours while Shane watched whatever he wanted. It wasn’t like he was some prisoner. He could’ve moved out from under Ilya, swapped in a pillow or something.

He wanted to know what was going on so bad. He wanted to ask was that your family on the phone? Do you like them? Do they like you? Why does it sound like you’re being pulled apart by the seams every time you talk to them? Maybe it was unfair because he’d only ever heard Ilya talk to his family twice in private settings. He discounted the draft because everything about that day had been a chaotic blur of nervousness and disappointment at going second.

“You haven’t,” he said slowly. “I like having company.”

“Ah,” Ilya answered. “Even stinky company like me?” He made a point to smell his armpits and recoil. The humor felt strange and off-putting.

“Yup.” He tried and failed to be casual about it, watching Ilya’s expression fracture after less than a minute of putting up a brave, if self-depricating, front. “I want to be here. With you, I mean,” he clarified haltingly. “Where and how doesn't really matter.”

The blanket fell to the floor, forgotten as Ilya dropped back onto the couch beside him. His eyes were hard, his mouth twisted in something that looked small and shameful, like a child caught doing something they shouldn’t.

“My brother called,” Ilya told him. “He wants what he always wants.” He exhaled harshly. “Money.”

Nodding, Shane tried to read between the lines but decided whatever he’d come up with would probably not be right, so he settled on an inadequate, but no less heartfelt, “That sucks.”

“Yes,” sighed Ilya, “It does.”


—


Shane expected Ilya to drive as fast as Shane would allow him to through the backroads of cabin country. Ilya did not. He drove a respectable ten kilometers an hour above the speed limit. They'd gone into town not for Ilya to visit a bank—You do not have banking app? It is twenty-twenty-one, Hollander, oh my god—but rather for cigarettes.

Not rolling his eyes when he found out the purpose of their trip into town had been some kind of red flag for Ilya, like Shane was allowing him to have them out of pity. Personally, Shane thought that was stupid. He thought smoking was stupid, too, but he understood that for a lot of people it was a social and cultural thing. Like drinking, which Shane only did very fleetingly, in social situations. He liked to think that a couple years in Europe had made him a little more accepting.

“Do… Do you want me to yell at you for it?”

“You always did.” Ilya paused. “You shouldn't smoke, Rozanov, is bad for you, Rozanov, makes your mouth taste worse than ass—”

Shane shoved him in the Shopper’s parking lot.

Ilya laughed his way back to the car, carton of cigarettes tucked under his arm. “What? Is true! You like eating ass. Is like special treat.”

“You're ridiculous,” Shane said, smiling. He didn’t argue the point for obvious reasons. “I don't want you to get, like, lung cancer. Obviously,” he shrugged. “But I’m not going to throw a fit if you smoke, Ilya. Might chirp you a bit when I outskate you, though.”

“Me?” Ilya pointed to himself. “You could never. I smoke to even play-field. Playing field?”

Shane nodded.

“Yes, this.”

His satisfied expression made Shane smile.

Ilya did not drive them back immediately. He steered them onto new roads, ignoring the GPS, bent elbow stuck out the window. He didn't drive the speed limit (no one really did, not even Shane) but rather by feel: they went fast enough to feel the wind but slow enough to keep the windows down without the reverb of road noise. They stopped a couple times to watch deer in a field, Ilya pulling off to illuminate them with his high beams, grass and rocks kicked up at the Merceces’ undercarriage.

“You should be more careful with your car.”

“Is your car, I am just borrowing it,” he corrected.

“You bought it.”

“You like driving it and I like you not driving boring jeep.”

“I do not,” Shane protested, even though Ilya wasn't completely wrong. “Your other cars are just too fast.”

“For these roads, yes,” Ilya flicked ash out the window. “If we retire here I will want three wheeler or something.”

Shane forced himself to play it cool, ignoring the way his heart kicked at the prospect. “That sounds dangerous.”

“Ah, but I was going to say big chopper with the handlebars,” he held out his hands to indicate how high they'd be and laughed at Shane’s expression.

“At least one hand on the steering wheel please, for fuck’s sake.”

Still cackling, Ilya put his hands back on the wheel even as he argued, “Is straight road. Nobody is on it but us.”

“Still. What if a deer crossed in front of us? He paused, watching Ilya's eyebrows creep higher, then went in for the kill. “Or a loon?”

“Oh fuck no,” Ilya shuddered. “If loon jumped out at us I would hit it. Splat.”

Shaking his head, Shane said, “You can't run over a loon. They're protected. There are laws.”

“Laws to protect shitty bird?”

“I mean, they're protected under like, federal law so yeah, I guess. You shouldn't hit them anyway, even if you're scared of them. Especially not with my car.”

“Oh, so now it is your car.”

“You said!”

Ilya looked over. His eyes were warm, even in the dark. Shane could just tell. He took Shane's left hand in his right and traced over his knuckles. “I did,” he said, pleased. “I will share with you. If you want new car, I will take you shopping during season. Harris would film it for social media. People would love it. You would hate my choices but I would have secret option for boring safe car and not a Ferrari.”

“Oh yeah? What kind of boring car would you buy me?”

“Mm, I will have to think about this. Whatever is most boring car with best safety rating. Maybe instead I put big bow on this one and hire someone to do decals so it says Rozanov and my number on the doors. Oh, and Centaurs logo on hood.”

“That would be so tacky.”

“Yes,” Ilya took a drag of his cigarette. “But funny, especially if people think it is your car.” He thought about it a moment, exhaling smoke. “You would be bad at prank war if we started one. I would have to tell you in advance. I bet you hate surprises.” He was smiling.

“I do,” Shane admitted.

On one well documented occasion, the Metros vets had decided to throw him a birthday party his rookie season, except they hadn't made the playoffs and the party had been thrown a month early. He hadn't seen it coming and had staved off the panic long enough to grimace through the photos and the singing and a single bite of a cake before retreating to the showers. It wasn't their fault Shane was different, but nobody ever tried it again.

Ilya hummed. “I will make sure there are none. They are good people. I know you have only met them once, but—”

“I know they are. They love you.”

“Not like you do,” Ilya pointed out.

“Better not,” Shane said, which earned them a smile.

“It would be funny, to play on rivalry,” Ilya said. “I will still want to beat you on the stat sheet.”

“Me too,” he agreed, because he did.

Some part of Shane had never forgiven Ilya for going first overall, for beating him back in 2008 at World Juniors, for dazzling the scouts and convincing them that he was the best player in their draft year. He had a feeling Ilya felt the same when it came to Rookie of the Year. Things that couldn't be changed, where there was only one shot and no chance to catch up, they had a way of staying with you.

Disappointment was good, in a way. It made you hungry. It made you push harder, made you want to find your limits and smash past the things that limited you. Shane felt like he had been doing that his entire career, right up until Sweden.

In Sweden, he'd been the best. It wasn't a contest. Sure he played hard, and it wasn't like he didn't have an off night here or there, but even at his worst he could feel the gap, even if he never acknowledged it. It had felt like overcorrecting: The Metros had made him feel like he wasn't enough, wasn't living up to the expectations they had, the investment they'd made in him. Växjö had just been happy he showed up. The room was better—kinder, more intelligent, more cohesive—but it had instilled in Shane a fear of backsliding, waking up one morning to find he'd become complacent.

Even if he never really thought he'd end up coming back to the league, there was still the Olympics, which had been a joke considering MLH players were barred from participating, and, well, maybe Shane had secretly thought it wouldn't be forever. Some delusional, foolishly hopeful part of him had thought that the Metros or the League would reach out and try to make amends.

“Are you thinking or,” Ilya made a swirling motion with his index finger.

Shane blinked. His sightless gaze had been focused on the deteriorating yellow reflective paint dissecting the road ahead into two uneven lanes. “Honestly?”

“Obviously, yes.” Ilya snuck a glance at him.

“I was just thinking that I'm really looking forward to being back. I know it will be different, but—”

Ilya didn't make him say it. “You were so bored. You want an actual challenge.”

He nodded and set the truth free. “Fuck, I was.” He felt himself blush, feeling bad about being honest. “I'm a little scared that I just jinxed myself for training camp, but—”

Ilya blew a raspberry. “I watched you in playoffs—” he cut himself off to wave Shane off before he could say something about it. “Yes, I was very sad and pathetic, Svetlana made fun of me for insisting to watch you play instead of taking nap before games.”

“That's—you weren't pathetic, Ilya.”

Another plume of smoke was sent out the open window. “Mmm, no, I was. She said I was like teenage girl with unreturned—requited? English is stupid.” He sighed. “I was like girl with crush, all heart eyes like little puppy dog, but I was watching. You are still good. Will be an adjustment, a little, but you are still the best in the game. I would not lie to you. Not about hockey.”

“I don't think you'd lie to me about anything,” Shane blurted, then flushed even harder.

They rolled to a stop at a T-intersection. Ilya indicated left, toward the cottage, toward home, but the car didn't move.

“Not on purpose,” he said gently, eyes focused on the stop sign straight ahead. “Sometimes I think I am struggling, but I don't know,” he let out a breath. It sounded heavy. “Is hard, sometimes.” He gestured with his hands, like a shifting scale. “I do not mean to be difficult.”

It was strange to come around full circle to what had begun all this, but also not. They had spent so much time talking—having sex, really—instead saying anything to one another that it was all bound to leak out eventually now that they were actively trying to communicate.

“You're not.”

“I am,” Ilya looked over at him, eyes a little glossy. He wasn't crying or anything, but his eyes caught what little light there was in the dark.

Shane frowned, trying to figure out what he meant. So much of what he had originally thought Ilya to be… wasn't. There was a difference between what Ilya showed the world, the league, the fans, the game, and the man Shane knew from stolen moments across a decade. This Ilya, his Ilya was not lesser. If anything he was sweeter, more genuine. Maybe a little too accommodating, but that could be the newness, the fear that this was a fever dream and not their new reality. Shane felt that way, too.

It was hard to be open with someone, even someone you loved. Shane was kind of lucky that his business had been made public: Ilya (and everyone else for that matter, though maybe not to the same extent) knew all about his struggles. The darkest, scariest parts of himself were mostly out there, for the world to see.

He got the feeling Ilya wasn't like that, that he held a lot of the dark things inside him, shoving them down, down, down until he could pretend like they weren't there at all. Ilya would hate to know it, but it kind of reminded him a whole lot of Derek, though they were a different kind of avoidant. Derek didn’t have the same cocky facade to hide behind, not like Ilya did.

And unfortunately, Shane wouldn't know, hadn’t known, until he was told, not that Ilya owed him that. It was just hard to watch the people you care about hurt. And Ilya was hurting, even if neither of them knew how to put it into words.

“I think,” Shane scratched at the faintest impression of stubble on his chin. “I think all we can do is try to be honest with each other. About what we think, and maybe how we really feel? Even if we don't know how to say it.” He shrugged. The chirp of crickets outside the car was nearly loud enough to rival the purr of the vehicle’s idling engine. “And if we fuck up, we fuck up. Together.”

“Together, huh.” Ilya looked over at him. “You make it sound easy.”

“Can't be too bad if it's you and me,” Shane pointed out. “Besides, I have crappy days too. Like, meltdowns and shit. I guess, just, I’ve got you and you've got me?”

Ilya nodded. “Okay,” he said softly, like maybe he was convincing himself more than he was reassuring Shane.

He pressed on the gas and they continued toward home.

“So, um.” Shane cleared his throat, unable to let the silence settle between them just yet. “If you ever want to talk, about whatever, you can. And if you don't want to talk or you just need, well—whatever it is. If I can give it to you, I want to. For the record.”

For a while, it was only the sound of the road and the whisper of rushing air around in the cabin.

Then Ilya sniffed and said wetly, “You are a terrible person, Shane Hollander. You come back in my life and bam! I am crying all the time.” He shook his head, as if he could will the tears away.

“I'm sorry,” Shane said, because he didn't want Ilya to cry if Ilya didn't want to. Personally, Shane felt a little embarrassed if he cried, but usually afterward, it was worth that minor discomfort. Crying tended to make the world a little more manageable.

Ilya's lip trembled as he patted Shane's cheek. “Is okay,” he said, letting Shane pry his fingers away to lace with his own before setting their combined weight in Ilya's lap.

Chapter 8

September, 2021
Ottawa, ON


“You're unbelievable,” Shane said, shaking his head at Ilya, who was shotgunning a diet coke like his life depended on it, free hand fanning his face dramatically on the other side of the kitchen island. It looked very stupid and ineffective and Shane was equal parts fond and exasperated.

“You are trying to poison me, Hollander,” Ilya replied, exhaling sharply before burping from the carbonation. “Is all a part of your evil plan to become best hockey player on my team.”

“It’s my smoothie,” he protested, taking a sip of it. It tasted fine. “You’re the one who wanted to try it.”

Ilya went to the fridge to get water, gulping down half the glass before refilling it from the little dispenser built into the door. Apparently the coke hadn't helped. “It’s like if sadness was a liquid, but also, like, spicy? Should be studied in laboratory.”

“It’s a totally normal shake,” Shane dropped a straw into his bright green breakfast and made a show of sucking down a bit of it. “It’s healthy.”

“Only shake I will be having is protein shake with milkshake flavored powder or actual milkshake. Motherfucker,” Ilya commented to himself, sticking his tongue out and waving at it. “My tastebuds will never recover.”

“You’re such a baby,” Shane rolled his eyes at Ilya’s pouting expression. “It’s literally protein powder, greens, and ginger.”

”What the fuck is a green? Like lettuce?”

“Kale,” Shane deadpanned, “Or spinach.”

“And you like this?” Ilya’s expression was somewhere between shocked and horrified.

Shane rolled his eyes for what had to have been the fifth time that morning. “It’s fine. Been part of my training regimen for years and it works, so,” he shrugged. “Don’t worry, I won’t try to like, convert you or something.”

The relief on Ilya’s face was noticeable. As noticeable as the look on Shane’s when he pulled a box of Count Chocula cereal out of the cupboard and poured it into a massive bowl. “Oh no, you don’t get to give me shit for eating happy cereal.”

“Like you didn’t just give me shit for my smoothie?” Shane countered, eyebrows lifting in silent accusation.

It felt good to be able to banter, like exercising a muscle that hadn’t been properly activated in a while. They’d spent a lot of the summer on tenterhooks, trying to be as considerate as possible of each other as they dove head first into a relationship. Neither of them really did things by half measures, which was really good in most ways and a little bit not so great in others.

With summer training and camp not so far off, it meant they needed to figure out a more stable normal: the place where Hollander and Rozanov became Shane and Ilya, what parts of them were owed to others because of their careers and what belonged to them alone.

Because of this (or maybe in spite of it, in Ilya’s opinion), they decided not to train together. It was weird, after nearly three months of waking up and doing mostly everything together, to be on different schedules. It was good, though. They couldn't live in each other's pockets forever, though Shane was pretty convinced both of them would happily do so if it had been a realistic option. Time apart was healthy.

They did try to have breakfast together before they went on to face the day, even if it was ten minutes of Shane standing around the kitchen drinking his post run smoothie while admiring a sleep-rumpled Ilya who grumbled about vegetables and checked out Shane’s ass in his running shorts when he thought he wasn’t looking while eating half a box of children’s cereal meant for Halloween. It was hard to be the first one to leave, to kiss Ilya goodbye and huff at the taste of sugary marshmallows, even if he knew they’d be back together by dinnertime most nights. In a way it made Shane look forward to the season even more. Sure they’d be busy, but they’d be together almost all of the time.

It wasn’t like Ilya was waiting around for Shane anyway. He had commitments: a handful of brand deals, some organization related items before the team’s internal and community outreach obligations began in earnest, and Luca.

At first, Ilya had tried to convince Shane that Derek and Luca would probably explode with joy if they all trained together, once he remembered that he had agreed to workout with the Centaurs’ up and coming star and couldn't follow Shane around all summer. Shane hadn't been particularly interested in that. He and Derek were particularly alike in this way, which was why they had wound up splitting off into two groups: Shane and Derek, Luca and Ilya.

It was nothing against Luca or Ilya or all of them together. This—summer training—was Shane’s time to hone his mind and body, to prepare it for the work ahead. Training in a duo was fine, but trios were louder, more messy. He shuddered at the thought of training in a quartet. That was better suited for camp, or maybe in the lead up to camp. He wanted to make sure he could stand on his own, that he came into camp ready and at the top of his game, not coasting in on some half-forgotten legacy dredged up by allegations and the media that surrounded it.

He knew some of that was anxiety, but it was still true. He knew it was going to be rough, in the beginning. Possibly the whole season. He’d have to prove himself to his new teammates, to their coaches and the organization, to the fans, and the rest of the league. It was part of why training with Derek was perfect, as they both had something to prove: Derek was on the last year of his deal, and he'd need to perform if he wanted to make more than the league minimum going forward.

”We are still meeting for lunch, yes?” A pair of arms wrapped around Shane’s middle, hands skimming over his hips as he rinsed out his smoothie cup and straw at the sink.

”With Derek and Luca?”

“Double date,” Ilya breathed against his ear. Shane shivered. “Who knows, maybe we will make headlines again.”

“I don’t think people realize they’re dating, so I highly doubt they’ll realize we are, too.” Ilya’s hands, which were stacked one atop the other around his abdomen, encouraged Shane to lean back into the embrace. “People keep spotting us at random places and commenting about ‘turning the rivalry into a brotherhood.’”

”Is ridiculous. We were arguing about how much kale you needed for smoothie of sadness in grocery market. Is peak couple shit!”

Shane tilted his head, letting Ilya get a good glimpse of his smile. “It is,” he agreed, “But tell me you don’t love it.”

Ilya shoved his nose into the juncture of Shane’s neck and shoulder, nuzzling there. “No,” he mumbled against Shane’s skin, “I cannot do this. Maybe they will ask me if I know who Lily is during camp when media doesn’t know what else to talk about but gossip. I will have to think of good answers.”

“Menace.”

“You like it,” Ilya’s hands fell to his hips and drove their pelvises together, back to front. “Do we have time to—”

Shane turned off the sink before flicking water into Ilya’s face with wet fingertips, laughing at the startled look of betrayal that flashed across Ilya’s features. It only grew louder, blooming into cackling peals as Ilya growled playfully, manhandling him toward the bedroom.


—


Shane had made it to the arena loose and happy, for obvious reasons. Unfortunately, that feeling didn't last. The anxiety crept in, made his mind buzzy and his fingertips tingle with restless energy.

That was fine. He’d planned for this, knowing it was inevitable. He just needed to get it over with already.

He'd made the decision to come out to the arena, rather than the practice facility a week before camp to reacclimate to the enormity of MLH arenas. It wasn't just the difference in ice size and the way the game changed because of it. It was everything else that came with it. Being on this ice would make it real, would confirm that he was back. It would make that first shift, first faceoff, first goal tangible.

This day had been looming over him ever since they’d come back from the cottage. He tried his best not to think about it, but that simply wasn’t his way. He’d considered asking Ilya to come with him, too, but in the end he'd decided he needed to do this alone. Whatever happened with Shane’s career was his own. He wanted to compete with Ilya, against him in the ways that two teammates could. If this was a moment of weakness for him, he’d rather push through that barrier on his own.

For all the world thought he had to prove to them, Shane knew he’d always had a whole lot more to prove to himself.

The dressing room was, as he’d asked when he’d booked the time with Coach Wiebe, empty. He laced up his skates and strode down the tunnel with purpose, stick in hand. He did one last check just to confirm nobody was around to see him. Harris, the team’s social media guy and one of Ilya’s close friends, would definitely want to get this moment on camera. If this had been before, Shane probably would have let him, too. It would be great marketing content.

Shane Hollander 2.0—what they referred to him on TV and in news articles, a wildcard of a man who was either playing for a lot less than what he was worth or had realized he wasn’t what he used to be—was more selfish than they made him out to be. That was a good thing. At the end of the day, he had to come first. He reminded himself of that a lot.

So: boundaries. He’d happily participate in content and do whatever he could to help promote the team within reason. He’d maybe not do the bits like Ilya and Zane Boodram did where they showed off their hobbies (Shane was a boring person, but he knew what a thirst trap was and he would not be posting videos of himself doing yoga anymore), but he was happy to engage with fans and do outreach or whatever.

These moments, though? The big ones? Those were just for him. Maybe the people close to him, too, sometimes, but yeah. Shane—Shane 2.0 or whatever they called him—was okay with being a little bit selfish.

The arena felt both smaller and bigger than he remembered. Ottawa’s arena was right around average, seating just over eighteen thousand people, more than three times the size of Växjö’s arena. It dwarfed even Malmö’s, which put the rest of the SHL to shame. And yet, the ice felt so small, almost claustrophobic surrounded by floor to ceiling seating.

The upper bowl felt so far away from ice level, more sharply angled, like it was a part of the ceiling or the sky than the building itself. Shane stared up at it from the bench, a sharp voice in his head reminding him that he rarely paid attention to them even when the stands were full of people cheering for or against him, so what did it really matter now?

Maybe it wasn't so much that it mattered, but that things were different now. The questions he was asked in his post signing media availability, the ones that would be asked of him in camp and beyond, until the world moved onto whatever it perceived to be the next biggest scandal, would be personal.

Did he think he still had what it takes?

Did he feel the pressure to perform for others like him? Had knowing he would be a pioneer in this League been a part of his reason for pressing formal charges against it?

What did Lily think about all this? Was he happy with where Shane wound up?

Shane had been spotted with several notable players this off-season—

No. There were some rules that even the media abided by. He'd allow them to ask if Lily was happy with his choices (Ilya was), but he would make sure the media coordinators knew that he would not be answering any questions about Lily's identity or any potential queer players in the league.

There were more than a couple, after all, and not all of them were on the Centaurs or Scott Hunter.

He shook his head until his face felt a little sensitive and gripped his stick until he felt the strange creak of his gloves against it. Without preamble, devoid of forethought, he threw himself over the boards. There was no clean re-entry, no open gates, only barriers he'd risen above. There was sense in putting it off.

The ice was pristine, marked only by his blades. He did a full loop at a comfortable pace, maybe somewhere just above quarter speed, taking in the familiar sight and smells, everything feeling like it was ratcheted up by a factor of a hundred. It was more than a little overwhelming, and he couldn't deny the heavy drum of his heart.

He swung around and came up to center ice, coasting in, blades angled perfectly to slow him down. He allowed himself to stand there, not in position for a faceoff, but as a man who never thought he'd have this ever again. He imagined the arena full of fans in their horrible red and black jerseys, imagined the wall of unintelligible sound.

As a child, he'd wanted to play for the Centaurs, because that's the hockey he'd grown up on. By the time he was twelve, it had become clear he had what it took to go pro, so with his mom’s encouragement he'd taken a more analytical approach. He threw himself into listening to AM radio feeds out of Montreal that crackled with static, an added challenge on top of rapid fire Quebecois French he’d only practiced with his dad at home. His mom made sure he was media trained, taught him how to say something while meaning almost nothing at all, employing all the tricks of the trade from a career working in telecom.

He had made himself into the perfect specimen, the kid you'd want on the ice and off of it. He'd been malleable, he'd adapted, he'd packaged himself to be fit for anyone's consumption, and look where it had fucking got him.

Cutting an angry figure eight, he came back to the dot. Lower this time, his stick poised just shy of parallel to the ground, he let his mind wander to some other time and space, to what a game would feel like. It was surprisingly easy to send adrenaline skittering through his veins. He lifted his chin, envisioned an enemy squaring up for the draw—

—and wasn't particularly surprised that his imaginary opponent was himself in blue and red.

Before, it would have been Ilya in his mind’s eye: all swagger and blinding smile, eyes narrowing with mirth and a chirp ready to fire as he licked his lips. He'd been everything Shane wanted and everything he wanted to rise above, all wrapped into one infuriating person he couldn't help but love.

He wasn't sure if it was a good thing that his biggest challenge was himself. Maybe the washed up players turned analysts on Sportscentre were right: maybe he wasn't what he was.

But there was only one way to find out.

Chapter 9

When the first trill blared overhead, Ilya and Luca went off like twin rockets. They kicked hard, propelling themselves down the ice with sharpness and speed, leaving the rest of the players in their group to trail after them. Derek, who'd fallen in with Shane when their names were called, shook his head.

“Don't know why I'm surprised,” he muttered, following at a much more controlled pace.

Matching him stride for stride, Shane hummed. “Different styles,” he offered, though it probably wasn't style, but rather ideology.

“Taking your time over there, Hollander?” Ilya's taunt was playful, but his eyes were sharp. He'd already turned back, waiting for the next beep to signal that it was time to return to their starting position, indicated by cones that made a dotted line at each zone’s faceoff dots.

“Slow and steady wins the race, Rozanov,” he called back dutifully, crossing through the cones and turning swiftly, ready to begin again.

Ilya blew a raspberry. “Boring,” he commented, making some of the guys watching from the bench laugh in a combination of amusement (veterans) and nervousness (rookies).

This was the first time Shane and Ilya had shared ice in years. It was also the first time since the 2009 MLH Prospect Combine since they'd shared it without being on opposite sides. So, second time total, but also first time where the goal was to play on the same team rather than to see who would come out on top.

It was more exhilarating than Shane had been expecting. He had a feeling Ilya felt similarly, considering how much he'd been chirping him since yesterday when camp had officially begun.

Another beep sounded and their group was off again, streaking past the benches and the two other groups gathered there in white and black jerseys with the Centaurs’ logo on them.

“Ignore him,” Wyatt Hayes said to Shane’s left. “I appreciate the company. I’m trying to make it to level eight this year.”

“He’s great for pacing,” Derek called around him. They crossed the blue line and spun around, putting their backs to the other group, distinguishable only by their black jerseys. Their group was in red.

“You two do this a bunch or something?”

“All the time,” Derek said to Barrett, who was on his other side, two down from Shane. The beep cut through his next words: “Hollzy made me do beeps every month in Sweden.”

“Then why the fuck did you only make it to level nine last year?” Boodram shouted from the bench as they skated by.

“You only made it to level nine?” Shane demanded when they’d made it to the other side. “Every month?” Hayes blanched. “That sounds terrible.”

“I was nervous!” Derek blurted before addressing Wyatt, “And it was!”

“There is a lot of chatting and not a lot of skating happening,” Ilya yelled at them. “This isn’t fucking social hour.”

“Sorry, cap,” all of them muttered in not-quite-sync.

Shane wasn’t the kind of guy to bet on things, but he was relatively certain that Ilya’s glove was in front of his face to hide his grin at Shane’s deference.

The chatter stopped after that, the guys locking in at their captain’s insistence. Ilya and Haas still went on like lightning in a bottle, the rest of them trailing after them. Shane started to notice Hayes flagging early in level seven—he wasn’t the only one, but he’d said he wanted to get to level eight, so—

“Hazy move your fucking feet,” Ilya barked without looking over. “I can hear you panting from here.”

There was no way he could. There were a couple guys who had hit the boards and dropped when they’d missed the last beep.

“Fuck you, Roz,” Hayes huffed, sliding in just in time to be sent staggering back the other way. The panting was only obvious because he’d been talking while skating.

“Stay on me,” Shane quietly encouraged. He was good at these kinds of tests. They just made sense to him, made him count the space between seconds in the sound of his blades serrating the ice, the number of breaths he needed to take. He loved the feeling of his lungs expanding with effort, the way it came on slowly, made his entire body feel buzzy and electric. “I’ve got pace.”

“Thanks, man,” Hayes didn’t tuck in—this wasn’t a race or anything, but he latched onto Shane’s pace for the next six laps before making it back to the far side and bumping his blocker with Shane’s glove.

He only made it two more shuttles before dropping out, but he seemed satisfied with his result. He’d met his goal, after all.

“Do I need to carry you, too?” Shane teased Derek, whose gaze remained firmly ahead in their rest between level eight and nine. The thirty second rests started feeling shorter and shorter as time went on.

“I’ve been keeping pace with you just fine,” Derek said. “Weren’t you supposed to be the nervous one?”

Shane liked the teasing. He shook his head and added an extra burst of speed to his next lap, just because he could, bridging the gap between himself and the Haas-Rozanov duo.

“You have decided to show up all of a sudden?” Ilya drawled, smirking.

“I thought this was supposed to be my comeback tour.”

“Oh, now you are comedian?”

Shane grinned, chewing on his mouth guard. He hadn’t had a good partner to chirp in years—not that he was particularly skilled at chirping, but he had always known how to get under Ilya’s skin when he was playful like this. “Nah, just waiting for some competition.”

The bench rippled with quiet murmurs, little oooh’s and chatters.

From center ice, the sounder went and they were off again.

Ilya had said to them in warmups that he wanted them to be hungry. He wanted them to push and push and break through their limits. He wanted them to fight. Shane understood that. It was the kind of thing he’d said to his team too, once upon a time, albeit not with the same vehemence and charisma that Ilya had. He understood his place now, that it wasn’t front and center—at least, not like that. It was foundational. He was meant to be the backbone, not the loudest voice in the room.

“I bet you could beat him, you know,” he said to Derek, words shielded from Ilya by his glove at their next rest.

“Fuck off,” Derek said, breathing hard, but not overwhelmingly so. He had another three levels in him, of that Shane was sure.

“He’s probably fallen off without anyone to challenge him,” Shane pressed, just loud enough to piss Ilya off.

“You go to Sweden and now you have chirps,” he scoffed. “Cute.”

“Guess we’ll see,” Shane threw back.

They skated hard for the next minute, dropping more guys at the top end of the tenth level than in all the ones previous. It was only the five of them left: Troy Barrett in the center, Ilya and Derek flanking him, Luca and Shane on the ends.

“See what?” Ilya asked, continuing their previous conversation. He didn’t sound terribly bothered by the skating, which was unfair considering Shane was now intimately aware of just how much he smoked.

“If I’m chirping or not,” Shane told him, matter of factly.

“And they think I am asshole,” Ilya huffed. “You are asshole. Do you guys fucking hear this?” He hollered in the direction of the bench.

“It’s about time somebody put you in your place, Rozy,” Boodram said merrily, sitting on the boards, legs dangling as he watched. “Best SMAT we’ve done in ages.”

“I’m going to make you do them monthly like Hollander did to Briggs since you like them so much,” he threatened Bood.

“If it makes us better,” Bood called back, ignoring the concerned murmurs of roster guys and prospects alike.

The next few levels flew by. Shane tuned out everything but the droning beep, thinking of other things, but mostly nothing at all. This was meditative for him in the same way most cardio was: Everything and nothing mattered, all he had to do was go down and back, rinse, repeat, keep moving, keep moving, keep moving, then recover well.

He came back to himself to a muttered, “Eight,” to his right.

“Stop counting, Briggs,” he corrected sternly, barely having enough time to utter the words before they were off again.

“Fuck,” Derek responded, choppy strides loud with only three others left. Barrett had dropped two shuttles earlier, not quite clearing the halfway mark of level thirteen.

“Eyes up,” Shane told him as he trailed in behind him. “You’ve made it to fourteen before. You’re going to today.”

Derek might make him a liar. Shane was pretty sure he only had another three to four shuttles before he broke, but Luca Haas wasn’t faring much better, and he wanted the two of them to push each other. Haas had yet to top out. He had some growing to do and a higher ceiling than Derek when you thought about circumstances and development, but Derek was stronger than he gave himself credit for. He hadn’t come close to his ceiling, either.

When Luca didn’t quite make it to Rozanov’s side before the beep went two shuttles later, Derek surprised Shane by pressing on.

“Don’t look so fucking suprised,” gasped Derek, words wheezing a little. “You said I’d make it to fourteen.”

“I did,” Shane agreed, swinging around him so that he was between him and Rozanov.

“Someone’s been putting in the work,” Ilya said approvingly. “This is your fault?”

“Nope, all him,” Shane deflected. He was sure he was a good influence on Derek, but the work that he put in was all his own. Shane didn’t want credit for it. Derek had worked hard to find his way to the show, and he deserved praise for it.

All three of them were still breathing hard when the next beep rang out.

“Let me show you how to do this,” Ilya called, streaking toward the dot.

“Motherfucker,” Derek swore, following suit, Shane beside him, then slightly ahead as the beeps grew closer and closer together. He made it a shuttle more before failing, doubling over and breathing hard when the beep went without him making it.

And then it was just them. Shane and Ilya. Hollander and Rozanov.

A romantic sportscaster or journalist would probably call it destiny.

In the old world, the one that Shane thought of as the one he’d left behind when the Metros did what they’d done and things splintered off, his life’s trajectory flung in a strange, sideways arc that eventually leveled out and went forward: things would play out pretty simply. There’d be a beep that was too close to call, Shane possibly making it, Ilya less than a quarter-step behind him. They’d argue over it forever.

In this new world, the real one, Shane was most recently a star player on a team that was good, but kind of had him to blame for a decent chunk of it. Sure he played less games a season, but he’d taken his team to three straight championships, carried them there on his back it felt like, not that he’d ever admit that to anyone except maybe Ilya, a long, long time from now. He’d averaged a staggering thirty one minutes and forty-six seconds in ice time over the course of those playoff bouts, and his regular season number was only about a minute lower.

He knew Derek must have said something because someone, maybe Chouinard? One of the older guys in white from the defensive group, called out, “What the fuck do you mean he played thirty three minutes a night?”

“I’ve seen him play thirty-five,” Derek clarified unhelpfully.

“Ignore them,” Shane forced out, the both of them barely through the cones before the beep sounded off.

Ilya rolled his eyes, but Shane was pretty sure that the grimace he’d had on his face was meant to be a smile, y’know if it wasn’t for all the physical exertion. “Ice hog,” he teased.

“Fuck you,” Shane replied, then turned on the jets. It wasn’t like he had a whole lot left in the tank, but he could keep going for a little bit. Maybe he’d drag Ilya to level fifteen kicking and screaming.

He didn’t. Ilya failed to reach the second to last shuttle of level fourteen, Shane completed the level, and instead of making Shane push on after the rest, Coach Wiebe called it while the rest of the boys sticktapped.

“You’ll be lucky to get your number’s worth of minutes,” Ilya huffed at him. “A bit more stacked than Sweden, yes?”

“I’m okay with that,” Shane answered, similarly out of breath.

“And just because you won—this time,” he was quick to clarify, “Does not mean you are taking first line from me. You can skate and hold stick, big deal. We will see if European leagues have made you soft.”

Coming from Ilya, the chirp didn't rattle him. “They haven’t,” he argued back, “But whatever.”

Their banter was pretty mild, heatless and fun to engage in even if it was mostly performative. Shane had been very clear about where he wanted to end up in the lineup. Most good teams had a top six that was interchangeable anyway. That didn’t stop Coach Wiebe from skating over and confirming there weren’t any problems.

Everyone only saw the rivalry, Shane realized as camp went on. If they were too friendly, people—most people, with some notable exceptions—were suspicious, waiting for a scuffle to break out, a couple of guys always ready to shoulder between them. It was like that night they'd all gone out to Monk's was a lie.

Maybe the boys had gotten so wasted they didn't remember Shane and Ilya leaving together. Maybe they thought it was coincidence? An act? Some strange, temporary truce? Shane couldn't tell and it probably wasn't worth metaphorically spinning himself in circles trying to figure it out. Maybe their coaches thought they were playing nice in pursuit of a cup.

It was stupid, really. They weren't fucking wrestlers. Their competitive drive was real, of course it was, but even when they pissed each other off, there was a level of respect there (even if you needed a fucking microscope to see it on Ilya's end in those early days).

Shane voiced this to Ilya when they left together on the third day of camp—together. For the third time. People really just saw whatever they wanted. Sometimes, Shane couldn't figure out why they didn't get caught. Other times, it was, well, this.

Ilya, sat in the passenger seat, looking content and maybe a little exhausted lit a cigarette before he leaned over and ruffled Shane's hair in a way that would send him squirming if it had been anyone else.

“Coach is very concerned that we will kill each other,” Ilya told him on an exhale. “Which is funny. Orgasm is called little death in French. You know this? I bet you didn't.”

Shane shoved him before putting the car—truck? SUV?—vehicle in reverse. “I know about le petit mort, Ilya. I knew that in like grade seven.”

“I bet your cheeks turned all red when people talked about it. Or maybe you didn't know about it at first? I bet you didn't. You were probably so innocent—”

“I mean, I was playing hockey with fourteen year olds,” he pointed out. “I heard plenty in French and English.”

“Anyway,” Ilya continued on, puffing away on his cigarette, “They worry about fit and if we will kill each other and yet, so many little deaths between us.”

Shane rolled his eyes at the innuendo, pointedly ignoring the molten lurch of arousal in his gut. “Are you not tired?”

Laughing, Ilya said, “I am going to take ice bath then crawl across house and take nap.” He smirked roguishly. “Then, we will fuck before DOMS arrives to kick both of our asses.”

“You're ridiculous.” It sounded perfect.

Ilya looked over at him, looking boyish and severe all at once. It was a contradiction but it suited him well. He looked happy, Shane thought, but almost like he wasn't sure he was allowed it.

Shane didn't let himself think. He reached over and took Ilya's hand, pressed a kiss to his knuckles. “I love you,” he said, the words smudged against the top of Ilya's hand. Ilya's smile grew more earnest, less restrained.

They waited in silence for the light to change.

Let the world think what it wanted, he told himself. It didn't matter. They were going to do it their way.


—


Unsurprisingly, Shane made the team. He wasn't allowed to play preseason, which he argued about but ultimately conceded was the right call when he realized two of the games were against Montreal. Most of the bones of that team were gone, but some notable guys from Shane's time remained, trapped between relevance and retirement in the way a lot of guys in their late thirties were.

The only difference was that the guys on Montreal's bench had been very clear about where they stood on matters.
