People wanted a piece of him: news outlets and sponsors and fans, all of which had their own demands. They wanted to know about his decisions, about Russia, all the little details he’d only scratched the surface of in their press conference getting carried into pre and post game sessions. What did his family think? Did he have anyone back home? Did they accept him?

It had been a fucking nightmare for watch Ilya go through.

In hindsight, Shane definitely should have seen this coming.

Ilya had bad days sometimes. Shane had quickly discovered he couldn’t always predict them. Over the summer there had been fun, lazy days swimming off the dock followed by days where Ilya was half present and foggy, sleeping for ages or unwilling to move from the couch or bed, where he either didn’t have much to say or snapped at Shane over stupid things as if testing to see what kind of space he’d be given, if he could actually drive Shane away.

Sometimes Shane could figure out it was coming by watching the curl of his shoulders or the tightness in the corner of his eyes, but—

But, at least in Shane’s experience, it had never been this bad. It was completely normal, and again, he should have expected Ilya to have a bad day. It wasn’t great, and Shane certainly didn’t want him to feel this way, but there wasn’t anything wrong with feeling low sometimes, right? Except, this wasn’t just a bad day. It was a barely get out of bed for practice and games but also maybe not sleep for at least thirty-six hours kind of day that maybe became a day and a half. The team wasn’t playing great either, which certainly didn’t help.

And then Ilya had one of these days, then another (and another and another and another and—) back to back.

Shane definitely wasn’t panicking about it to their friends in a separate group chat.

Ilya didn’t seem to notice the state he was in until a morning halfway through the month. Shane hadn’t been able to get him out of bed for morning skate, so he’d messaged coach to say they weren’t feeling well but would rally for the game tonight and hoped he wasn’t lying. When Ilya woke up it was nearly noon, and Shane was still in bed with him, sitting up against the headboard, book in hand.

He flinched when he saw the time, looking up at Shane without commenting on his glasses—which scared Shane, if he was honest with himself—and murmuring a soft, “We have game today?”

Shane hummed an affirmative, and Ilya rolled onto his back from the curled up ball he’d been on his side of the bed with a muttered curse. “I texted Wiebe that we weren’t feeling great but we’d rally for the game. He said to check in by four or text medical if we needed someone to come to the house.”

All of it just kind of slipped over Ilya. The first part registered, but the rest seemed to be too much detail.

The worst part of bad days, well, when Ilya realized they were happening, at least, was the way he apologized, like he was doing something wrong by feeling the way he did, like he could somehow control it. Shane was pretty sure nobody asked to feel the way he did, and Shane didn’t know how to fix it. He’d do anything it took to make it better, if he did. Unfortunately, it didn’t seem like Ilya knew what to do about it, either, so they were left with a single option: to wait for the dark cloud that seemed to hang over him to pass.

“There is something wrong with me, I think,” Ilya said quietly.

Shane wasn’t sure he’d be able to hear his words if he’d flipped the page of his book at the same time.

“Probably. I don’t know. I feel,” he exhaled, and fell silent, dragging himself over to the other side of their bed to rest his head in Shane’s lap like it took all the energy he had to do so. “I don’t know the word for this. Heavy? Is not right, but,” He sighed, out of words.

Shane shoved his book on the nightstand without a bookmark, stacking his glasses on top of it. His hand found Ilya’s hair and smoothed it around his ears. “It’s okay if that’s how you feel.”

“It fucking isn’t,” Ilya snapped. “You don’t have to show up in six hours and be the captain of our fucking team—” he seemed to realize what he was saying, or more importantly how he was saying it as he spoke and promptly cut himself off. “Sorry,” he shook his head into the meat of Shane’s thigh. “I’m sorry.”

“S’okay,” Shane assured him, swiping at a tear that had broken free and trailed sideways down Ilya’s cheek and mentally revised that this was in fact the worst part. Ilya cried like he was losing a battle, like it was a catastrophic defeat, not a release.

“It’s not.”

“I’m not going to take it personally,” Shane clarified. “What can I do to help? Stay? Give you space? I could leave—”

“No,” He sounded panicked by the prospect. “Stay. Always stay.” His arms came around Shane’s waist awkwardly considering he was halfway sprawled across Shane’s lap and twisted awkwardly in the bedsheets.

Shane wriggled his way out of the embrace, righting the bedsheets and pulling Ilya halfway on top of him. He did not smell great, but at the knob of his shoulder and the line of his stubbled jaw, he smelled less like unwashed body and more like home. He mentally rebuked himself for trying to remember if it was yesterday or the day before when Ilya had showered last, recalling that he had rinsed off after their last practice but not well enough and the sheets definitely needed to be washed, but it wasn’t important right now. “Okay, well, now you’re on top of me and I can’t go anywhere. What else?”

Ilya made a sound into Shane’s bare chest that sounded a lot like he didn’t know, but he was shoving his face between Shane’s pecs and nuzzling there, taking hot, humid breaths against Shane’s skin. Shane smoothed back greasy curls when they made a valiant effort to get into his mouth and nose before resting both hands in a loose clasp on Ilya’s back.

Unsure of what to do—and unwilling to research it not because he didn’t care but because it was a gateway to overstepping and doing more harm than good (Svetlana’s words, backed up emphatically by Hayden at six this morning when Ilya had finally fallen asleep for the first time in nearly two days), Shane made the only neutral suggestion he could think of: “Do you think maybe this is something you should talk to Terry about?”

The nuzzling stopped. Ilya was immediately defensive, lifting his head. His eyes were dull and the bags beneath them weren’t as dark as they’d been, but Shane could still see them. “Terry is medical doctor, not fucking therapist. What is he going to do, huh? I won’t take fucking happy pills, whatever the fuck you call them, so why bother?”

Mentally channeling Svetlana’s encouragement not to rise to the bait and get defensive back, he let the words sit between them for a couple seconds longer than he’d have liked to, tipping his head back so that Ilya was forced to look at his chin and not glare into his eyes.

“Well,” he began slowly, eyes trained on the orange peel texture of their bedroom ceiling, “He could help you find someone who is, if you thought talking would help.”

“If I wanted to talk,” Ilya argued, “I would just talk to you.” He shifted on top of Shane as if to pull away, cross and ring bobbling against Shane’s chest as he lifted his head, but when Shane’s arms stayed firmly closed around him, the resistance had him wilting. It was clear he wasn’t actually trying to escape, but rather confirm he was being held, that Shane wasn’t going to let him go even if he was mean.

Shane was pretty sure he’d never forgive himself from walking away from Ilya all those years ago. Every time Ilya tested him like this, it felt like someone took a hammer to his ribcage. Even so, Shane would bear it. He deserved to remember this feeling, just like Ilya deserved to know he was worth it.

“I mean, you can talk to me, if you want.” Shane was pretty sure he didn’t, not because he didn’t like, love Shane or anything, he obviously did, but because it was hard to put your thoughts together and then spit them out at someone and expect them to just… take them on. It was why Shane was the kind of guy to plan most things and dial in on routines rather than float nebulously through his existence. He needed a morning run or an evening yoga flow to shove the stupid thoughts from his head and make what he wanted to say somewhat sayable. “But to be fair, I don’t exactly speak your language. Not that you’re not great at speaking English, but—”

“Is not easy to explain in Russian, either.” His brow furrowed as he pressed a frown into Shane’s chest. The animosity had abandoned him. A begrudging sort of weariness had taken its place.

Shane shrugged. “It’s probably easier though.”

“You will not say that when therapist says depression is genetic,” Ilya retorted flatly, “That I am maybe a little too much like my mother—” He stopped himself with a microscopic shake of his head, pouty lips parting. “I—”

It was like invisible goosebumps prickled Shane’s skin from head to toe, or maybe he’d licked an exposed wire or—or something. Everything felt white, tingly, electric. Fear warred with panic in a battle he would ultimately lose if he didn’t get himself together immediately. His jaw worked and words didn’t want to come, but Shane knew he couldn’t let Ilya take this back.

“Ilya.”

Ilya didn’t answer him, too busy turning his head to look away.

“Ilya,” Shane spoke sternly, “Look at me, please.”

“No.” His voice was thick. “Sometimes I think you would be better off without—what the fuck?”

Shane had flipped them without thinking about it, rolled Ilya underneath him, elbows planted on Ilya’s chest, hands firmly cupping his face. Ilya blinked up at him, stunned.

He really hated that he knew what Ilya was going to say, the way he’d wanted to finish that sentence.

“There is no way, in any fucking universe,” It was like Shane was both whispering and shouting all at once, his voice an odd combination of quiet and serious, “That I would ever be better off without you. So you tell whatever fucking whatever in your head has you thinking that to fuck off because that’s just not true, okay?”

His face crumpled as he spoke, and the words themselves were inadequate, barely scratching the surface of the things Shane would say if he were a more eloquent person. By the end of it, his lips were trembling ever so slightly more than his hands, which were hooked around the edge of Ilya’s jaw, and he could feel the sting of tears welling up along his lower eyelid, threatening to fall.

“Okay,” Ilya murmured, one of his hands coming up to swipe at the tears that inevitably spilled down Shane’s cheeks before pressing it over his necklace, the engagement ring that hung beside his mother’s crucifix, pushing the weight of it into his skin. “I will talk to Terry.”

Shane blinked. More tears slid down his face.

They laid there for a long time, mostly just holding onto each other, occasionally trading half-spoken words of apology and devotion until the other shushed them. Shane thought about how Ilya had admitted just how dark of a place he’d been in those months Shane had kept his silence, about the way Cliff and occasionally Hayden called Ilya and didn’t hang up until he laughed, how Svetlana had shown up after the father’s trip, announcing that she’d gotten an unexpected holiday and decided she wanted to skate the canal and she’d already picked out which guest room belonged to her in the basement so she wouldn’t have to listen to them have sex, how Wyatt always seemed to know when it was time to watch Deadpool for the eightieth time instead of whatever latest Marvel show he obsessed with.

Shane wondered how long it had been going on, if it had just felt manageable before for the most part, or if like Shane, Ilya was agreeing to try and fight for himself because he finally had something he wanted to keep, someone who wanted to keep him, someone he wanted to horde for himself because their broken edges only matched each other’s and it was agony to be separated.


—


The Centaurs did not play well in February. They had lost four games out of their last five. It wasn’t quite catastrophic yet, but they were missing out on much needed points in the standings coming up to the trade deadline and something had to give.

Ilya had pulled himself together somewhat, a combination of time, the team and organization rallying around their captain, protecting him from media whenever possible, and the weird boost that sometimes came with taking action. He’d met with two therapists, vetoed the first outright and reluctantly agreed to meet the second in person when they returned from their weird one-off roadie in Vancouver.

Vancouver was a different kind of beast. They were having a bad season and they were miserable with it. The game should have been over before it began, yet it festered. The first period felt like it had been two hours, like they should have been back at the hotel already.

Their opponent was playing angry. Bood called it the low and slow kind that had been given time to grow teeth, become hungry. They couldn’t win the regular way, which meant things got ugly.

And of course, the Centaurs weren’t exactly playing the greatest hockey themselves: a combination of injury, a long season, and everything else that came with it. Ilya was still struggling, but he hated being handled with care by anyone but Shane, so he’d taken to aggressively overcorrecting in the locker room and on the ice. He played kind of like Vancouver did: pissed off at the world and sloppy because of it.

It made sense that Wiebe started mixing up the lines in the second, trying different combinations to see what would spark. First up was swapping back Haas and Bood, because Barrett could keep up with Ilya, when he went full tilt, but Luca wasn’t ready for how gritty and aggressive his style of play had become. It wasn’t a mark against him, but rather a mark against Ilya, who was playing with the kind of devil may care attitude that could get him injured if he wasn’t careful.

Their play didn’t get worse, but it didn’t improve, either. Wiebe kept it up, tapping shoulders, changing things up on the fly. Shane got thrown in with a couple of third liners, LaPointe and Goodwin, both of whom were fine, but not really able to keep up with him. Vancouver didn’t really know what to do with that, and Shane was pretty sure he could keep it up for another minute so he shook his head when he skated by with the puck and Wiebe slapped Ilya’s shoulder to send him over the boards on Shane’s wing.

They had run lines together in practice, spent a lot of time working power play drills. It would have been stupid not to, even if the plan was not to regularly play together until necessity demanded it or playoffs required the team to step it up a level. This was not intended to be that, but rather a quick double shift for Shane before he swapped out for whomever the coaching staff dubbed to be next.

He took the puck into the zone, dodging the winger that tried to pinch him at the blue line, dropping it back to Ilya, who flubbed the pass. Bood grabbed it, but the puck had gone over the line and they had to tag up. Shane’s shift was over, one of the assistant coaches tapping Derek, who came out the gate as Shane hopped up, straddling the boards.

Derek carried the puck in this time, leaving it at the top of the slot for Ilya to collect. Young ended up with it in the corner, fighting with two of the Vancouver players to come out with possession. He managed to throw it to Holmberg, who had taken Bood’s place. Bergy sauced a beautiful no-look pass to Ilya, who charged the net, shouldering through traffic to get a shot on goal that was ultimately stopped by the goaltender and covered up for an o-zone faceoff.

Ilya circled the net as the whistle went. Valkov, Vancouver’s top centre, grabbed his sweater and pushed him into the corner, and the obligatory scrum broke out.

The linesmen separated them quickly. Ilya had mostly grabbed and held Valkov, his expression drawn. Scanning the rest of the guys out there, Shane saw Derek come in close to his captain, but Ilya shook his head, so he backed off. Neither team changed, so it was Valkov and Ilya in the dot.

Valkov was running his mouth. Shane couldn’t hear what he was saying, and he was pretty sure it was in Russian, but the referee blew his whistle and leaned in. There was no mistaking what Valkov said next. “Since you’re no longer Russian maybe I should say in English, huh? Soft fucking fa—”

The referee should have called a penalty for unsportsmanlike conduct, but plenty of shit was left to stand on the ice, especially if the broadcasts couldn’t hear it. The puck dropped as usual. Ilya won the faceoff, hurtling the puck diagonally across ice for Chewie to collect while he broke left for the opposite faceoff dot. He got the puck back behind the net, and Shane saw Valkov accelerate, lining up the hit.

So could Derek, who turned on the jets, dropped his man, and plastered Valkov to the boards just beside Ilya with the force of a freight train. Shane saw Derek’s face as he’d lowered his shoulder and knew Valkov was quickly learning that Derek had earned his nickname. At the same time, Ilya rang the puck around the boards and up to Bergy, whose shot wound up in the goaltender’s glove, not that anyone was paying attention.

Valkov had gotten back to his feet and thrown his gloves to the ice, making a beeline for Derek, who’d left him to collect himself. “Rozanov is not man enough to fight anymore, eh?” he was shouting. “Game is too hard for weak little faggots.”

Derek whirled around, got one glove off and popped Valkov in the jaw. His head snapped back. Derek shook the second one free, grabbed the collar of Valkov’s sweater, and proceeded to beat the absolute shit out of him. When he finally went down, Derek was on top.

“This faggot just kicked your ass,” he snarled as the linesman pulled him off Valkov. It took the other man a moment to struggle to his feet, “So what does that make you?” He tried to come back for more, and the referee swooped in to prevent a second altercation. Derek managed to jab a finger into Valkov’s chest all the same. “Keep his name out of your fucking mouth.”

Fights were a part of the game, one that when leveraged effectively, could get one or even both teams going. There was something primal about it, a show of dominance that lit up parts of their hindbrain, that weird animal part of a person and made them lock in, refuse to cede ground from that point onward because whatever was going on was so tense and charged that someone had thrown hands about it.

In this case, it backfired spectacularly for Valkov and Vancouver. Valkov went down the tunnel with five for fighting and a game misconduct, the zebras finally realizing they had clocked in tonight and needed to work the game. Derek would miss the rest of the second period and about a minute of the third, but Valkov was done for the night, which meant Vancouver was short their first line centre. Ottawa’s lineup was far more versatile and had no shortage of guys who could switch between forward positions if necessary.

Derek remained unnaturally coiled and still as he was directed toward the penalty box, expression frosty as Ilya herded his discarded gloves in that direction with his stick, Derek’s tucked under his arm. They exchanged a few words when he got there, which left both of them shaking their heads ruefully as Ilya glided back to the bench.

Wiebe was waiting. “You good, Roz?”

“Yes,” he replied with an annoyed sounding sigh, like he couldn’t believe he’d been asked. To be fair, a lot of teams wouldn’t bother. “Briggsy is too, if you were wondering.”

“Good. You’ve got forty seconds to breathe and I’m sending you back out.”

“I am ready to go now,” Ilya argued, but Shane hopped the boards.

“Too bad,” he and Wiebe said in unison, which had the guys chuckling. “What’d you say to Derek?”

“Ah, no.” Ilya shrugged a single shoulder. “Is what your delinquent son said to me.”

Shane could only imagine what Derek had said. It could range from tender to testy. Derek was unpredictable when angry. “Which was?”

“He told me we were trying new things tonight. Said he picked fighting, so I should try scoring a goal for a change.” Ilya was shaking his head again. “Can you fucking it?”

“What an asshole,” Shane commiserated, but he could see the slightest twinge in Ilya’s lips, like he wanted to smile, and made an executive decision to chirp the shit out of him as well. “It’s only been six games.”

Ilya’s surprised expression was photo-worthy. “Oh, now you are ganging up on me,” he glowered.

“I mean,” Dillon chimed in, closing the gate behind him and thumping Ilya on the shin with his stick as he skated toward the offensive zone. “Why the fuck hadn’t you thought of that, Roz?”

“Fuck you both very much.”

Tanner threw a gloved hand over his shoulder in a middle finger that wasn’t—it was impossible to properly make that gesture with gloves on. “Come on Hollzy, we gotta remind him what a goal looks like.”

“I cannot believe this,” Ilya was complaining as he finally returned to the bench. “You are all ganging up on me.”

Nobody denied it. A couple of guys were definitely pretending to talk to the assistant coaches so they could turn away or otherwise hiding their laughs behind sweaty gloves.

“I mean, it's fine if you can't keep up in the scoring race…”

“Shane Hollander. What did you just say to me?”

Shane gave him a cheeky grin. “You heard me, Rozanov.”

And that was the other thing the fight had done, the real reason why Valkov and Vancouver had sealed their fate by targeting Ottawa’s captain—it had drawn the Centaurs’ attention inward. They weren’t chirping Ilya because they thought he wasn’t performing, but because it was the distraction he needed to tune out what had happened. Shane had read the guys’ faces, seen the way Derek’s words had unlocked something in them.

Ilya was the heart and soul of this team and someone had thought they could get away with trying to hurt him. The Centaurs were going to utterly ruin them in their own fucking building.

Shane was absolutely sure of it.

Chapter 23

March, 2022
St. Paul, MN


The trade deadline came and went in the first week of March. The Centaurs didn’t do much in terms of movement: their lineup—at least in terms of offense—was stacked in a way it hadn’t been in years previous. Making the playoffs was all but guaranteed. The team’s only major announcement came in the form of a five year, four million dollar extension for Derek. They came out of their February slump while other teams floundered, making changes they hoped would propel them to games played after the second week of April.

Even though they’d gotten their footing, Coach Wiebe still kept at it with the fucking blender.

It was annoying to Shane, who preferred structure. He knew which guys he played best with, and the instability of that changing night over night grated upon him. He understood why it was happening. They needed to know what they had before the playoffs came and depth was necessary. Besides, the Admirals were having a historic season, having already clinched the eastern conference if not the league standings overall. Only Colorado came close, and Shane could already tell they wouldn’t overtake the Admirals in points. The New York team was determined to ride the momentum of last year’s cup, let it take them into dynasty territory if they could.

So Shane got used to playing with guys and providing detailed notes to the coaching staff afterward: Stafford’s puck handling was sub-par but he might work if his ice time coincided with a stay at home defenseman like Dykstra to collect his giveaways. Linden was too slow for Shane and just as reckless as Ilya. Charpentier, on the other hand, needed more games—the bones were there but it wasn’t a given that he’d be able to step it up if they called upon him after the first round when things got tight.

“Still at it?” Ilya asked, interrupting his train of thought. “It has been nearly an hour. You barely played sixteen minutes tonight.”

“Don’t remind me.” Shane glowered up at him over the rim of his glasses, displeased by the reminder. His minutes were being managed so that he wouldn’t go into playoffs tired, but it chafed, feeling an awful lot like coddling. His body felt good in a way it rarely did this late in the season. He didn’t want to lock up or go cold. “You can turn on the tv if you’re bored.”

Rolling his eyes, Ilya took his journal and shoved it into the side pocket of his duffel which was lying haphazardly at the foot of the hotel bed.

They were wrapping up what Shane had taken to calling homework, which they’d begun doing almost every night: Shane reviewing film and taking meticulous notes while Ilya did the journaling his third therapist had not so subtly recommended. Ilya had rankled at the prospect of it at first, telling Shane about how he’d nearly fired her on the spot when she suggested it because he wasn’t going to write down all his thoughts and feelings for her to dissect, only for her to say, rip out the pages and burn them when you are finished for all I care. It is not my journal. Paper has no feelings.

He pretended otherwise, but Shane was pretty sure Ilya liked Therapist #3 three quite a bit. It had been almost three weeks of her, the numbers lining up in a way that made Shane’s brain itch in a good way, and while Ilya tended to be tired and withdrawn after their sessions, they seemed to be helping. He was having more good days than bad ones. Maybe it was just that it wasn’t making things worse, but Shane was trying to think positive.

Therapist #3 had actually grown up in Russia, which meant she’d passed some kind of unspoken test. Two days ago, Ilya had even referred to her by name after their session, which in Shane's eyes was a glowing endorsement.

Ilya's chin hooked over his thigh, pulling Shane from his thoughts. “You are distracted,” he commented.

“Just thinking about you.” He set his iPad and notebook on the too big side table in his side of the bed and leaned down to kiss the top of Ilya's head.

“Mm,” Ilya rolled onto his back, looking up at Shane’s face. His legs hung sideways off the bed. “I am very sexy and distracting.”

Of course, Shane thought, not allowing the deflection. “I was actually thinking that you're really fucking brave.”

He groaned. “No, your line is, ‘Yes, Ilya, I am finished with my homework and need to suck your cock.’”

Shane smirked down at him. “Is it now?”

“Mhm, yes. Keep up,” he chided, eyes creasing at the corners in a secret smile.

He reached up. Shane grabbed his hand, pressed his lips to Ilya's knuckles, then sandwiched his hand between Shane's hand and chest, against his heart.

Telling Ilya that he was impressed with how diligent he was about working on his mental health would get shrugged off, but even before this Shane had always been impressed by the way Ilya applied himself to whatever he set out to accomplish, both on the ice and off it. He was a good person—the best, really, even when accounting for all the cocksure, assholish, arrogant parts—because of his imperfections. He was someone who tried very, very hard, even if he pretended otherwise.

“Okay, I’ll bite.”

“Kinky.”

“You are unbelievable,” Shane said, already slipping out from underneath Ilya’s head to slide down the bed and settle between his partner’s thighs.

Admiration could be shown in many ways and all that.

After, they returned to their previous position—Ilya using Shane's thigh like a pillow, though both of them were wearing far less clothing—Ilya peered up at his phone while Shane texted casually with Hayden, who was two hours behind and bored on the Raiders’ one and only California road trip of the season.

Ilya sniffed, which would mean he wanted Shane to pet his head again, but that sniff was followed by him rolling onto his back and smooshing his cheek against Shane's skin.

Instead of the pouting and sex-rumpled attention seeking look Shane was expecting when he looked down, Ilya was frowning at something on his phone. “Do you check your Instagram messages?”

That was an odd question. “I mean, I look at them sometimes, yeah, but not regularly.” Shane didn't do social media, but Ilya knew that. “Why? Did we, like, break the Internet or something?”

“Not today,” a smile tried to break through, but it didn't reach Ilya's eyes. “Have you looked recently?”

A thrum of instinct sent Shane's nerves jangling, discordant. “Should I?”

“I think so,” he murmured. “Could be nothing, but,” he trailed off, frowning at his screen again. “Does the name Mason Briggs mean anything to you?”

Ilya's eyes found his. Shane didn't even have to say anything. His face apparently said enough.

“Bylat,” he swore.

“What'd he say?”

“I was checking profile. I haven't looked.” Ilya levered himself upright and tucked himself against Shane's side. “Do you have one too?”

“No.”

“Did you check?”

“Don't have to.” Shane navigated to his Instagram account and pulled up the list of blocked users.

Ironically enough, user @mbriggs29 was listed right before @metrosmlh.

Ilya scoffed at the sight of the Montreal handle—probably mentally cursing the whole city and its terrible team—and tapped the message request. Someone else might say it sounded disbelieving, but Shane knew better.

He watched Ilya read the words on the screen, his eyes darting back and forth in aggressive micro movements, nostrils flaring in a way that suggested emotional control. When he finished, he handed the phone to Shane.

On the first read, it sounded nice but something about it nagged at him.

Hi,
Sorry for the weird message, but I'm trying to get in touch with my brother Derek, who plays on the Centaurs. It's been years since he's talked to our family and our parents and grandparents are getting older you know? What happened was years ago and I forgive him. Could you relay the msg and tell him to contact me? My number is 814-XXX-XXX. Thanks man.

PS: your one of my favorite players.

Shane read it twice more and tried to figure out what it was that bothered him. “The forgiveness thing is weird,” he eventually decided, instead of mentioning the lack of grammar at the end of the message or the weird, potentially manipulative ego stroking in the end note.

“That is one word for it.” Ilya's jaw flexed. “I think you should call Derek.”

Shane checked the hotel clock. It was four minutes off. He’d checked earlier. Still, “It's almost midnight.”

Ilya must have seen Shane's reluctance—yes, Shane was somewhere along the lines of concerned, angry, and unsettled by the development, but there was nothing to be done about it tonight. Even so, Ilya prodded at his phone, then held it up to his ear. It rang out, leaving them with the soft baritone of Derek's voicemail greeting.

“Call me when you get this, Derek.” He paused, considering something. “You are not in trouble, I just got a stupid message on Instagram from your brother.” He didn't bother saying goodbye, ending the call and depositing his phone on the bedside table.

Shane sighed. “He’s gonna freak out when he hears that.”

“I just told him he is not in trouble!”

“You first-named him.”

“You ‘first-name’ him all the time!”


—


Ilya covertly tipped his phone screen in Derek's direction while Luca asked Shane about his decision to fake a pass on the Minnesota goaltender over breakfast in the hotel restaurant. It was earlier than the continental breakfast was supposed to be available so Shane was drinking half burned sludgy drip coffee and eating a banana while he waited for the hot food to come out, Luca tittering softly in his mild accent as he reasoned through how Shane had read the play.

Thankfully Luca propped up the conversation easily himself, fingers of the hand not holding a pastry sliding around film from the game, trying to hone in on what Shane had seen.

Shane dutifully pointed out the subtle flick of the goalie's eyes downward paired with the way his shoulders were curved in—things that pointed to low confidence, which meant he was more likely to bite in an attempt to be defensively conscious—while Derek's face fell across the table. Ilya casually tapped at a couple things on his phone, Derek leaning in apprehensively.

To the world, it probably looked like a captain taking a player aside to give them some pointers or a more experienced linemate providing feedback, but all Shane saw was the blankness in Ilya's eyes and the tight line of Derek's shoulders that remained once his own expression smoothed out into something well versed at handling unpleasantness. It made his chest ache.

“Shane?”

Right. Now was not the time to get lost in his thoughts.

Out loud he said, “Shit. Sorry, Luca. Not awake yet.” It was a lie. He'd already been up and in the hotel gym, burning off anxiety on the treadmill while Ilya grumbled, pedaling on the bike next to him after refusing to stay in bed alone. He sold the bit by taking another sip of his coffee and leaning in, trying to be more engaged. “Say again?”

“Just,” Luca flushed. “Wondering how he compares to Renaud in Pittsburg. We only play them twice and I haven't figured out how to crack their goaltender. Analysts say they're similar.”

“Which analysts?” Wyatt had a beanie on, looking weirdly thin in joggers and a thermal shirt, a brandless black hoodie unzipped and partially hanging off of him. He pulled out a seat before he realized he'd have to get up to make his own coffee and hovered beside it instead “If it's Hank, fine, but the rest don't get a say.”

“Why not?”

Bood made a beeline for the coffee bar, waving Hazy into his seat. “Because,” he gestured to Hazy, “Only tendies can really comment on tendies. The rest don't know shit.”

Wyatt nodded approvingly.

Luca looked between them, nose scrunched with confusion. “Wait, so I should ask Hazy?”

“No, of course not,” Wyatt waved a hand, then stopped, distracted by Bood, who was dumping sugar packets into their coffee and realized how he'd sounded belatedly. “I mean, you can definitely ask for advice, of course, kid.” He tapped his cheek. “I just mean not to take analysts too seriously. You're in the game now, so rely on people who actually know puck. Like, the state of it now. If Hollzy says Renaud is like Parazza, then believe him. If he doesn't…” Wyatt looked to Shane expectantly.

Shane was happy to offer his opinion. “Yeah, that's bullshit. Renaud is more like Miitka in San Jose: Never learned to mind the gap between blocker and torso. Shoot low blocker, but if you make him think you'll go glove, he’ll drop early to try and play it so fake him out and you can tuck it in five hole if you're quick enough.”

“Câllise, Hollzy, you are so brutal,” Chouinard said, taking the vacant seat next to Shane. “Please do not tell me what you think about me, ever. I will assume you think I am traffic cone.”

Shane frowned. “Come on, I'm not that bad, I'm just honest.”

“Truth hurts, Nick,” Bood said, plunking Wyatt’s coffee down in front of him and rolling his eyes at Wyatt’s confession of eternal love and adoration. “But it's easier to hear from someone who wants you to improve.”

Luca nodded along. “Yeah, Shane’s been super helpful with drills and stuff to get my speed up. I still need to work on my hands.”

“Don't say that too loud,” Shane stage whispered, “Ilya will get jealous.”

“I have never been jealous in my entire life,” Ilya lied, dragged into the conversation because he could never resist denying any accusation that suggested he had ever experienced an emotion.

Shane raised his eyebrows.

Ilya did not raise his back, which was a sign to drop it. He went back to speaking quietly with Derek, angling himself toward the younger man, propping his cheek on his palm. With his elbow pressed to the table and fingers curled upward, his mouth was obscured. Whatever he said, Derek nodded along with.

A moment later, he stood, jerking his head toward the lobby. Derek followed suit. “I need something better than rubber eggs and miserable coffee. Bigrig has drawn short straw and will be gopher for donuts and whatever else I decide to gift you fucks.” He swept his gaze around the room, only to flinch as he made eye contact with a server carrying a hot pan full of scrambled eggs to the buffet table. “No offense.”

Naturally, the guys broke into jeers, booing him, but the server wasn’t phased. The younger man—probably Luca’s age, though Shane wouldn’t pretend to be a good judge of that sort of thing—dropped the tray into the chafing setup, looked back at Ilya over his shoulder, and shrugged.

“You're good, bro,” he said mildly. “People eat ‘em because they’re free. We know they're terrible.”

Ilya’s eyebrows practically disappeared into his hairline, but he recovered quickly enough. “See, he knows!”

Shane was almost a hundred percent certain the kid had no idea who he'd just called bro, so he muffled his laugh in his elbow, using his free one to text Ilya a request for an actual coffee to free him from the burnt sludge in the cup in front of him.

When they had gone, Luca nudged Shane with his elbow. “I know, Shane,” he murmured quietly, eyes still trained on the iPad. “Mason messaged me, too. I won’t pretend that I get it, but,” he let one shoulder drop in the impression of a shrug. “We’ve talked about it. I know family’s tricky for him.”

Somehow, it didn’t feel like the relief it should have been.
